NATIONAL INSURANCE.

HOW THE TORIES LOST A GREAT

OPPORTUNITY.

More Successful Ward Meetings.

If the provisions of the National Insurance Act are not thoroughly understood by the workers of Warrington it will not be the fault of the Progressive Labour League or of the Warrington Liberal Association which have been devoting much attention to the education of the constituency on the provisions of this great measure. Meetings are being held in all the wards and, after hearing the Act outlined, the audiences are invited to ask questions on any points which appear obscure. There has been a notable absence of hostility towards the Act, the questions having been directed from a sympathetic standpoint and with a genuine desire for enlightenment. On Monday evening the Progressive Labour League held a meeting in St. James' School. Latchford, the principal speaker being Councillor James Kendall, of Manchester. There was a good attendance. Mr. W. CLEG G, who presided, said the Progressive Labour League had been d3ing magnificent service for at least ten years. It had brought to the forefront of local politics many important questions, and some of the measures which the League advocated had now become the law of the land, although in the first place they met with ridicule and opposition. With regard to the unemployed, the Tory party formed miserable authorities called distress committees, and gave Town Councils power to appoint an officer. He was, however, utterly helpless, because he had no means of carrying on any work but inquiry. To-day, owing to the activity of the Liberal party. we had our fine labour exchanges. (Applause.) When Mr. Lloyd• George introduced his great Budget—(applause)—it was said that the country would soon he ruined and that capitalists would be leaving it. To-day, however, we had record trade returns and our rate of unemployment, which was only about 2 per cent., compared very favourably with any other country. The passing of the Parliament Bill bad given Liberals encouragement to krep plodding on, with the certainty that they would be able to pass many measures which otherwise would have been out of their reach. If there was one feature of the Insurance Act which particularly appealed to him, it was that part dealing with con- sumpti, n. When they realized that 75,000 persons died annually in Great Britain from that disease, it was an appalling condition of affairs. The only preparation that we had had in the past was 3,000 beds under private control and only the wealthier classes had been able to secure them when they had been required. It was, however, the lowest strata of society, the men with lowest wages and the poorest opportunities of securing good food and housing, who were most liable to be attacked by that dreadful scourge. We as a people ought to thank God that lie had raised up a man who had been enabled by extraordinary efforts to produce such an ameliorative measure. (Applause). The Tories had said and written a great deal about the Act, but it had not been with a view to giving a correct statement of its advantages. In 1883 the Germans, whom the tariff reformers were so fond of quoting, passed their first instalment of National Insurance. Since then they had had eight or ten Bills extending the scheme and to.-day they had a fait insurance measure. Their scheme, however, bore no comparison, from a workman's point of view, to Mr. Lloyd George's Act. (Applause). Councillor KENDALL said Liberals could afford to put the Insurance Act forward without any party glamour or any great amount of party claim. At the same time they could not forget that as the result of the want of appreciation of A SPLENDID OPPORTUNITY their opponents did not avail themselves of the cLan.ce of having a partnership on cheap terms in that magnifi- cent measure. The offer to make the Bill a non-party measure was made by Mr. Lloyd George, who invited the co-operation of all sections of the House in a great national scheme for the betterment of the workers. The offer was not accepted by the Opposition and the Liberal party had to take the responsibility of forcing the Bill through—be was willing to accept that term, because if a measure was good,the sooner they forced it through the better. If they had it forced it through they would have been fiddling with t now and in the meantime consumptive people would have bad to wait still longer for the means of being cured,whilst millions of workers would have had their enjoyment of the benefits of the Act suspended. The credit for the measure having become an Act of Parliament was due to the determination of its author and of the Govern- ment to place it on the statute book at the earliest moment. It was a remarkable fact that after an Act had been passed, speakers should be going about the country Co tell the people exactly what it meant. It was not because the Act, did not speak for itself. It was simple because PO many people were making the Act say something that was not in it, and there was so much MISREPRESENTATION AND APPEAL TO PREJUDICE that it had become necessary to explain what the Act really provided. The result would be that when the Act came into force it would not be regarded merely as a compulsory measure but would be backed by the goodwill of the people who would have had time to realize its objects. The Act was a great instrument for helping forward social work and he considered that be was doing as much Christian work by endeavouring to clear the path for such a measure as be had ever done in connection with a Christian Church or Sunday school. (Applause.) The Act recognized that the great need of the workers was to have some help in those times of difficulty which came to so many of them. There was nothing which had been soch a night- mare to millions of workers as the position of the steady workingman who realized that it only needed a week or two of sickness or unemployment to jeopardise his home. He (the speaker) was glad to see that the trade unions recognized in both portions of the Act something that was going to be enormously beneficial to their members, and the men might well trust their leaders in that matter. The Act not only provided insurance in time of sickness but it aimed to be an insurance of health—which was a much more important matter. He refused to believe that the medical men, whe had rendered magnificent service to the poorest of the poor, often without any hope of reward in this world, would stand in the way of a great scheme of social amelioration having a fair trial. (Applause.) Moreover, the terms offered to the doctors were a distinct improvement on what they were at present receiving for contract work. In future HUMAN BEINGS WOULD BE PLACED ON AS HIGH A PLANE AS MACHINERY AND HORSES and in the earlier stases of their incapacity they would receive attention which would in many cases avert a serious breakdown. One great fault of our industrial undertakings was that while there was an allowance for wear and tear and depreciation in respect of machinery, there was no similar provision for human life. In future the State would say "If an industry is wear fog out men and women—the most important, essential and delicate portion of that indaltry— some provision shall be made in the balance sheet in the form of the employer's contribution for the wreckage and wastage of human life in that industry." The speaker referred to the meanness of rich ladies who objected to pay 3d. per week for the insurance of a domestic servant. If Mr. Lloyd George had said : " The domestic servants must contribute sirpence and the State will pay the rest," those mistresses who were now objecting would have said "What a splendid man Lloyd George is ! Our thriftless girls, instead of having to be dependent upon us when sick, will now he made to contribute." Alluding to the "white plague,' Councillor Wendell said that PO long as we continued to make no attempt to deal with consumption, we as a nation were annually committing 75,000 murders, because it had been discovered that those lives rould be saved if we only adopted proper methods. Under the new Act the State would recognise its duty to the poor man's child and the anfferer from consumption would be taken away from depressing conditions and treated amid fresh air and sunshine with the best that medical science could devise. The scheme would be free from any taint of charity.(Applause).Having shown bow the employers would gain through State insur- ance by having healthier workers, Councillor Kendall said he bad yet to learn that there was any organised opposition to the Act on the part of the workers. They saw in the measure a means whereby they could help themselves by their combined thrift and effort. The bulk of the workers did not want to be carried; they wanted to be allowed to walk. (Applause.) Several questions were then asked and answered. Mr. E. NV YNNE JONES, in proposing a vote of thanks to the speaker and chairman, emphasised the fact that the Act was going to be a great preventive of sickness as well as an insurance against it. He could not under- stand why their opponents should try to make so much of what they considered the weak points, instead of looking at the great benefits of the Act. Difficulties would crop up but great power was given to the Insurance Commissioners to meet hard cases and to modify difficulties. Their opponents made an attempt when the Bill was passing through the House of Commons to take that power from the Insurance Com- missioners. Mr. Worthington Evans moved an amend- ment to prevent the Commissioners having power to modify the Act, but fortunately it was defeated. The Act was the greatest measure of social amelioration that had ever been passed by this or any other Govern- ment. (Applause). Mr. HARRY NEW seconded. He pointed out that under the Act the owners of slum property—of which Warrington bad no small share—would be made responsible for excessive sickness caused by their neglect. Anything that tended to bring home responsibility in matters of public health was a step in the right direction. The Bill would also do something to combat the terrible rates of infantile mortality in populous centres. In this connection Mr. New pointed out that in Hampstead, which was ore and not over- crowded. the death rate per 1,000 of infants was much lower than in congested Southwark. People ought not to view such questions merely from the .fi s. d. point of view, but should be

aAMI;

===== 0002601_19120302_art0016.txt | included=true | included =====
prepared, if necessary, to make sacrifices for the 1 general good. He had, however, yet to be convinced that workingmen were called upon to make any sacrifice under the Insurance Act. There was not a single clause that was detrimental to their interests. He appealed to Liberals, Tories, Socialists and all industrial workers to give the Act a fair trial and he was convinced that at the end of twelve months there would be no need to defend it. (Applause.) The vote was heartily passed.

MEETING IN WHITEGROSS WARD. THE COACITRIKE.

Miners' Moderation Commended.

A highly successful meeting promoted by the Liberal Association was held in St. Barnabas' schools, 13ostock- street, on Wednesday evening. Alderman James Evans presided and was supported on the platform by Councillors ff Roberts nnd J'Hawthorli, Messrs T Ternent (Rochdale), H G Roberts, J Elwell, H New and J. Dutton.

The CHAIRMAN opened the meeting with a reference to the cloud which vim over-hanging the industrial world. He believed he was expressing the feelings of the majority of workingmen when be said he was sorry that in these days of advancement in many directions we were not able to settle disputes on better lines than by striking, lie had had some experience of strikes and he had never known one that was beneficial. It might be that sometimes the workingclasses had gained something but, looking at the subject all round, they had also lost something. Apart from that, be believed that in this age there could and ought to be better means of settling disputes than those of warfare. Force was not a remedy and they must adopt some more satisfactory basis if England was to maintain her posi- tion amongst the nations of the world, lie fervently hoped that in the course of a few hours peace would be declared. (Applause). Councillor H. ROBERTS moved a resolution cordially welcoming the National Insurance Act as a measure that would be conducive to the welfare of the great mass of the workers. Alluding to the coal strike, he said be could not refrain from paying his testimony to the moderation of the manner in which the workers' representatives had conducted their side of the negotiations. If a similar disposition to meet the difficulties by discussion and con- sideration had been shown by some of the employers weeks ago, instead of at the last moment, he was positive that we should not be praying for the dissolution of the black cloud which over-hung the industrial world but that it would have passed away long since. Whatever else might he said, it was due to trade unionism to say that the position of the worker to-day was very largely attributable to the efforts made in years gone by by the advance guard of the working- classes who saw that combination in regard to their

attitude of the doctors, the Act gave sufficient power to come to terms witk them, and they were now in a more reasonable mood. He believed they would eventually come into the scheme and do their duty. The Act was the finest which had been passed by the British Parliaments—it gave benefits "pressed down and running over" such as no friendly society could at present give. In twenty years we would wonder how ever we did without a measure which was destined to transform the whole society of our country. (Applause). The resolution was curried unanimously. Mr. J. ELWELL moved a vote of thanks to Mr. Ternent for his address. As a worker he rejoiced that they had such a man as Mr. Lloyd George, who was able to see visions of what a beautiful country England would be if we could abolish the dread diseases and evils from which the workers suffered. The Act would help many a hard-working man to tide over troubleous times. (Applause). Mr. H. G. ROBERTS seconded. He said that in two or three years time, when the Act was in complete operation, the Tories would be sorry for the part they had taken in by-slections in opposing the scheme. (Hear, hear). The vote was heartily carried. A similar compliment was paid to the chairman and other speakers, on the proposition of Mr. HARRY N seconded by Mr. DUTTON.

TORY ELECTIONEERING.

MR. LLOYD GEORGE ON INSURANCE ACT STORIES.

ma.r'•"

Mr. Lloyd George has sent the following message to Mr. F. E. Hamer, chairman of one of Sir Arthur Haworth's meet logs in South Manchester who had called the Chancellor's attention to assertions that shop assistants and similar classes would be worse off under the Insurance Act than they are now : " I heartily wish Sir Arthur Haworth a triumphant return to the House of Commons. The answer to the misrepresentations to which you refer is that in a case where a workman at present gets nothing during illness he will in future bevel% guaranteed sum to rely on, in addition to free medical aid. "In a case where at present he receives wages during illness, the allowance of the Act will increase his resources at the very moment when he is most in need. " I cannot believe that any employer will deprive his workmen under such conditions of any privileges they j are now enjoying. The German employer did not do I so in similar circumstances and I refuse to believe that English employers will be behind Germany in their treatment of their men. " Be assured that the English Church in Wales will get full justice and liberal treatment in the Disestablishment Bill. The Church itself, through its advocates, is deliberately refusing to make the slightest concession to Welsh national feeling."

THE COAL CRIkS.

The Government, during the week has made strenuous efforts to bring about a peaceful settlement of the coal dispute. Me.tings between the Premier, his colleagues and officials have been taking place daily. Our picture shows the Special Committee of Coal Owners on their way to Downing-street,

to lay before Mr. Asquith replies to certain questions addressed to them. On the left, carrying bag, is Sir Thos. Ratcliffe-Ellis, Secretary of the Mining Association, and next to him Mr. Davis, Chairman of the South Wales Owners.

===== 0002601_19120302_art0020.txt | included=true | included =====
interests as a body was just as necessary to them as it was to the capitalist. (Hear, hear.) Alluding to the Insurance Act, Councillor Roberts said the details were subject to experience and could be adapted to the requirements of the times, but be was there to support the general principle—and not to support it as an employer and not as a workingman. (Applause.) The speaker showed what a blessing the maternity allow- ance would have been in cases which bad come under his observation. The Tories, he continued, were doing all they could to delay the operation of the Act but the workers should remember that for every day it was delayed 2,700 mothers would be deprived of its benefits. When the Tories talked about Mr. Lloyd George's indecent haste to confer the benefits of the measure upon the people, he would ask them to go into the homes of the working classes and see the conditions under which life was born and had to be lived for the first few months of its existence and to endeavour to realize the worries and anxieties of parents whose means were insufficient to carry them through times of stress. He would be a hard and black-hearted man who would delay the operation of the measure in that direction for five minutes, let alone for a single day. The provisions for dealing with the awful scoorge.of consumption were in themselves sufficient to justify the acceptance ol the measure. The act was, indeed, A CHARTER OF THE PEOPLE as far as health was concerned. (Applause.) Councillor Roberts made an interesting reference to local aspects of the fiscal controversy, which will be found reported In another column. Councillor HAWTHORN seconded the resolution. He referred to the great suffering to countless families I which the last coal strike involved and said he hoped ' we were not going to have a renewal of that distressl in 1912, but that the commonsense of masters and men I would lead to the settlement of a long-standing dispute. When the Insurance Act was introduced, he continued, it was received with a chorus of approval from Liberals, Conservatives and social workers of all kinds. Since then the ardour of their Conservative friends had cooled and it now became necessary to deal with a great many representations with regard to the Bill. The difficulty to him was not to understand the details of the measure but to understand the gross misrepresentations of its provisions. It was a great measure, providing for insurance against loss of health, the prevention and cure of sickness and insur- ance against unemployment. Mr. John Burns told us that 30 per cent, of the poverty in this land was directly attributable to sickness, and to that we must add a further contribution in respect of unemployment. It was against those two great poverty-producine factors that Mr. Lloyd George's Act was directed. If given a fair chance, it would remove some of the difficulties which beset a solution of the social problem and would prove even more effective and beneficial than the great scheme of old age pensions. (Applause). The CHAIRMAN said that Warrington was in the fore- front, so far as sanatorium work was concerned, and he hoped Mr. Lloyd George would kindly remember the Warrington Corporation when he made his disbursement of the funds for dealing with consumption. (Aplause.) Mr. T. TERNENT then delivered a very interesting and comprehensive speech on the Act. He said that if the Tories had only half an eye they would see things as they really were. But that was the reason why men were Tories—they had not half an eye and could not see the true perspective of things.(Laughter.) The Government had appointed as Chairman of the Insurance Commissioners, Mr Astor, the Tory member for Plymouth. He had declared in regard to con- sumption "The great point is that a beginning has been made. The State has taken a step that can never be retraced towards the extermination of the scourga that .levies so terrible a toll on the lives, resources and happiness of every class of the communtty." lie (the speaker)was glad that Lancashire had done something to form opinion in regard to the maternity grant. In Rochdale they had an employer of labour, Sir George Kemp, M.P., who had an arrangement in his works whereby. every married woman who had been twelve months in big employ, on approaching maternity had three months holiday with an allowance of 12s. per week. (Applause.) We had arrived at a point when the supposed decadence of the race was a serious subject of discussion. The last report of the Registrar General showed the lowest birth-rate on reoord. That was a serious problem. If we were going to have low birth-rate, we must see to it that the children who came into the world were well cared for and nourished. That was the only way in which we could build up a strong nation. With regard to the

THE LATE PROFESSOR A. H. YOUNG.

, The funeral of the late Professor' A. H. Young, of Manchester, took place on Monday, at the Manchester Crematorium. The remains were afterwards interred in the family vault at Warrington Cemetery. The mourners were Mr J Rymer Young (brother), Mr Whately (brother-in-law), Mr Tangye, Professor Paterson, Dr Donald and Professor Thompson. At the Crematorium there were many members of the , medical profession and other friends. The Manchester University was represented by the Vice Chancellor (Sir Alfred Hopkinson), Professor Elliott-Smith, Professor Sterling, Professor R B Wild, Professor Arwid Johaunson, Professor Seaton, Professor Alexander, Professor Petavel, and Professor Perkin. Amongst others who attended were :—Dr Richmond, Mr L Greening, Mr J Longland, Mr S P Silcock and Mr F J Dutton, 'of Warrington. The service was conducted by the Rev. H. Anson, of Birch. The floral tributes were both numerous and beautiful, including those from the Anatomical , Society of Great Britain and Ireland, colleagues and ' friends at Victoria University, staff of Anatomy Department, Victoria Infirmary, and the widow and members of the family. Mr. G. Elliot-Smith, writing to the "Manchester Guardian," says :---`• In Manchester and the neigh- bourhood there are scores of friends of the late Professor Young who appreciate at their real worth the great services he rendered to the Medical School during the period of its most effective growth, his beneficent influence in developing the Salford Royal Hospital, and his efforts honestly to shape university policy on the lines which he considered rightfand proper. Scattered through England and the Empire there are hundreds of medical men who look back with feelings of affection and esteem to the man who not only taught them anatomy with quite exceptional success, but also gave them an example of a blunt manliness and honesty of par pose and of contempt for mean and underhand ways which few of them are lkely to forget.

===== 0002601_19120302_art0022.txt | included=true | included =====
LENTEN FARE.

There was much wisdom in the edicts of the early Christian Fathers who prescribed a restricted diet during the 40 days of Lent. Those astute old scholars knew full well that the populace was greatly addicted to the consumption of an excessive quantity of meat, and so it was deemed wise, not only from a spiritual, but likewise from the temporal standpoint, to reduce the amount of flesh meat in the weekly bill of fare. It is a matter of almost common knowledge that despite the absence of meat, the fasting community enjoyed the best of health and vigour during Lent. So it can be nowadays. Instead of pinning your faith too much on a diet of meat, just look around and see what good things Nature has provided. Take, for instance, the little Greek Currant, which is the best of all nutrients, full and powerful and lasting food. If you would be wise, as the old Law-givers were, try a liberal diet of Currants. Then you will appreciate the value of a certain amount of abstinence from meat. You will discover the wonderful properties of Currants, how lasting is its effect, how easily digestible it is, and that after all there are better and more savoury foods than the oft-recurring beef and mutton. There are so many ways in which Currants can be used that it is quite simple to intro- duce them into at least one meal a day. If the season of Lent teaches you something about the use of Currants, it will not have been spent in a vain way from a worldly point of view.

The New Offices.

The fortnightly meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians was held at the Workhouse on Wednesday, Mr G Mather presiding. There were also present :—Mrs Simpson, Rev A M Mitchell, Messrs E L Bent, A J Jolley, J NV Knowles, W Gill, J A Hughes, W C Brooks, W Flanagan, R Lee, NV Houghton, M Seton, W Furness, I.` Ritchie, A Brooks, A Bottomley (clerk), R W Peacock (deputy clerk).

-THI NEW OFFICES.

At a meeting of-il;e-Finance Committee the Clerk reported that he had interviewed the owners of the premises' Nos 19 and 21, King-street, Warrington, and stated that they were prepared to letthe premises to the Guardians on the following terms :—For a term of 21 years at a yearly rental of £95, the Guardians to have the option of terminating the tenancy at the expiration of each seven years, and with an option to purchase the premises at any time for the sum of £2,375,the Guardians to be allowed six months' rental from the date of actual tenancy if the premises are occupied prior to 25th June next or from this date if the premises are not occupied before, subject to the Guardians carrying out the internal decorations. It was recom- mended that the offer of the owners to lease the premises on the terms stated by the Clerk be accepted and that, subject to the sanction of the Local Govern- rnent Board and the Registrar General, an agreement he entered into to that effect. It was further decided

that Messrs. A. Brooks, A. J. Jolley and W. Furness be appointed a committee with authority to carry out the necessary alterations and also to arrange for the furnishing of the premises and to confer with the Clerk and Surveyor to the Rural District Council re the arrangement of the rooms that will be required to accommodate the Rural District Council staff ; also that Messrs. W. and S. Owen be instructed to prepare all plans and specifications that may be required by the sub-committee, the Local Government Board and the Registrar General. TAINTED PXNS.

Tainted runs are ;m7 injurious to the health of stock birds, and when fowls have occupied the same run for any length of time the soil becomes sour and ought to be treated. The top soil should be removed first. After skimming this off about one inch deep, fork up the run, and while doing so keep sprinkling into it a spadeful of lime. The fowls should be kept off the run for about three weeks so as to allow the lime to thoroughly do its purifying work. Grass 11.1138, of course, should not be dug. It is sufficient to simply scatter the powdered lime over the surface and allow the rain to wash it in. If poultry keepers would only pay more attention to the state of the runs they would be troubled less with disease amongst their stock.

RHODE ISLAND REDS.

717;

Readers will still have in their mind what was said of these fowls last season. I then wrote a somewhat lengthy aiticle on their merits as utility fowls. The above picture represents a pair of single-combed Rhode Island Red fowl. There is another variety differing onlyin comb—these are known as rose-combed 'Reds.' Both varieties are most useful fowls ; and when of a good strain they make excellent winter layers of good sized winter eggs. A useful farmer's and cottager's fowl—they are fast finding increased favour. As regards price, they are now within the reach of all. Sittings of eggs can be purchased so low as 3s. 6d. per dozen. TOWLH IN CONFINEMENT.

Year by year we keep adding to our list of back- yard poultry keepers. Those who are just starting need some advice, older hands need none, for it is amongst this class of poultry keepers that we find some of the most successful. The first consideration is accommodation. If your operations are confined to an ordinary sized backyard without any outside run at all, then you will have to be content with a very small stock. It is far better to keep half-a-dozen fowls in good health and laying condition, than to overcrowd your limited space with more. Remember then not to overcrowd. Having learned all the hints, you may extend at some future time, when the condi- tions as to space may be more favourable.

BREEDS TO KEEP. The novice is often bewildered as to what are the best breeds to keep. There are so many varieties that one does not wonder. It is even more difficult still, if one's fancy happens to be a heavy variety and the conditions and accommodation not suitable for them. It is useless keeping a pen of heavy birds in a very confined run, for however careful you might be in your feeding and general management, they soon become too fat to lay well, therefore content yourself with other tastes. The light and non-sitting varieties are essentially the backyarder's fowls. Colour also is another consideration, especially when your run is situated in or near a large industrial centre. This we will assume. The most suitable varieties then, would be any of the following :—Anconas, Black Leghorns and Black Slinorcas. If you can allow your stock a run outside the yard as well, then you could keep any of the following heavier varieties :—Croad Langshans, Black Wyandottes. Black Orpingtons, Rhode Island Reds and Barred Rocks. Of course if the appearance of the birds is a matter of indifference to you, then any of the light-coloured birds could be kept, for it is well-known that there are many other most useful varieties not mentioned in the foregoing list. You see how necessary it is to be guided by circumstances, or as one of my readers puts it "Use a bit of common sense," This a good advice, for what may suit one class of circumstances may not suit onother. Thus having grasped the main idea you can easily apply just a bit ot ordinary common sense.

FEEDING.

Fowls kept in confinement need special care in feeding. Here they are deprived of natural tit-bits in the form of worms and insects, and also gi een food. These can be substituted. For adult fowls give cut green bone and minced beef about every other day, loz. per bird, and one of the best substitutes for green food is

===== 0002601_19120302_art0030.txt | included=true | included =====
SPROUTED OATS.

It is only of recent times that this class of feeding has come into prominence, and many of our leading poultry men are using it freely. The following is a good method of sprouting the oats : Take a few shallow wooden boxes, with sides about three or four inches deep; in the bottom of these place a thin layer of fine ashes, then place your oats about one inch thick; the chief point to watch is warmth and moisture but do not get them too wet, or the roots will rot. It is a good plan to first soak the oats before placing them into the box, and then watering them freely will prove sufficient. In winter time, of course, it is more difficult to get them to sprout unless you have a greenhouse, failing this, they can be sprouted success- fully in a warm scullery. Sprouted oats are a splendid substitute for green food, and after they have grown to about two inches long they are greedily devoured and much relished, especially by fowls kept in close confinement.

OTHER FOODS. We have dealt with green food and flesh food, now of other food stuffs. The best grains for fowls in con- finement are oats, peas and wheat. Maize should never be given only in very severe weather. The soft food should consist of biscuit meal, middlings and pea meal. Always add a small quantity of table salt in the soft food, and in cold vet weather, mustard (as recommended in previous articles this season). The drinking water should constantly be renewed and occasionally you should adds piece of sulphate of iron therein. The grit box should always be well supplied, and a box of fine dry ashes should be placed in a corner of the run where dry.

THE SCRAP:IIING SIIED. There should always be a scratching shed added to the sleeping quarters. I; is not advisable to encourage the stock to frequent the sleeping quarters during the day-time. In a back-yard run the scratching shed is nearly always the only place where the fowls can occupy the time in scratching. The shed need not be large, providing it is well built and kept dry. On the floor of the shed should be thrown a good layer of chaff or straw litter, and as it becomes tainted it should be renewed. The evening feed of grain should always be thrown down in the litter. This forms good exercise for the fowls, and helps very materially to keep them in good, healthy condition.

TO-DAY'S CUF TIES.

- - - - ----- Cup-tie fever has not had much to feed upon in Warrington so far, and unless the Wirepullers surprise their best friends to-day there will not be much left of the football season to get excited about. The draw has been unkind to the locals in sending them a second time to Yorkshire, and with such formidable opponents as Hull Kingston Rovers. The "Robins," as they are called, are at present the second best team in the broad-acred country, and have not bowed the knee at Craven-street this season to any side but the Australasians. On paper, Warrington's chance is a very poor one.

But paper form is not a reliable guide to cup-tie football. It does not seem so very long since Warrington were due at Wheater's Field to meet Oldham in the final tie. The odds were considered to be largely on the side of Oldham, but Warrington won and with a bit to spare. That was in 1007. Two years before that they met their opponents of this afternoon in the final tie at Leeds, and there too they won, though on foreign soil. Unquestionably, War- rington were a stronger team then than now—Fish, Dickenson, and Brooks were at their best—but the Wirepullers have not lost the old cup-fighting spirit, and it is " dogged as does it" in cup-ties. They will journey to Hull with hope in their hearts and, who knows? they may compel a replay at Warrington. I think this is what most people are praying for more than anything else. Two years without a draw at Wilderspool is bard on "the faithful."

The men have been quietly training throughout the week, and with the exception of Bradshaw and Brooks are reported to be as "fit as fiddles." The two latter players have both been su eriiag from strains, but they are selected, and everyone hopes they will turn out. Waywell will make an excellent substitute for Bradshaw if need be, but the loss of Brooks would be irreparable. The Club is hard up for half-backs, and the forceful methods of the international are usually responsible for half the attack. With the whole team fit and well, Hull Kingston may find them a harder nut to crack than they anticipate. I need make no secret of the fact that at Craven-street they think they have a "soft thing on," in vulgar parlance.

My chief fear about these Yorkshire cup-ties is on behalf of the forwards. We have the lightest pack to-day which has done duty for the Wirepullers for many a long year. The youngsters are keen, fast and clever, but in cup-tie contests weight is an important factor. There is a danger of the Warring- ton pick being swamped in the scrummages, but if they can bold their own here, then the result should be close. The Rovers are not a brilliant side behind. with the exception of the full hack—a man with 92 goals to his boot this season. It must be Mclntyre's object to send the ball anywhere but to the hands of Carmichael, and the team generally must be cautious about the infringements. In goal-kicking ability the Rovers have the pull over any team in the Union, but it is opportunity that makes the goals. A word to the %ise should be sufficient.

The Warrington team has been chosen as follows: Mclntyre, full back; Bradshaw, Renwick, Dickenson and Woodburu, three-quarters; Daintith and Brooks, half backs; Thomas, Shugars, Cheaters, Tranter Skelhorne, and Fearnley, forwards.

Hull Kingston Rovers team will be : Carmichael, full back ; Brain, Phil Thomas, Hughes and Ascott, three-quarters; Dean and Surman, half backs ; Mann, Sandham, Huskins, Holtman, Blackmore, and Moore, forwards. Referee: W. McCutcheon, Oldham.

A apecial train leaves the Central Station for Hull at 9.10, fare 45., and a goodly crowd will probably cheer the Wirepullers on. It will be noted that the fare has gone up 3d.—part of the general increase in excursion fares imposed by the railway companies since the strike troubles and the increase in wages.

The other ties are as follows : Oldham v Wigan Hull v Rochdale Hornets Leigh v Huddersfield • Barrow v Batley Salford v Dewsbury Halifax v Broughton Rangers. Keigbley v. Wakefield. The winners should be Oldham, Rochdale, Hudders- field, Batley, Salford, Halifax and Wakefield.

At Rochdale the Warrington men played a game which was distinctly encouraging for to-day. Every man did well, and the forwards were particularly brilliant in the loose. The team did not deserve to be beaten pointless, but the absence of Brooks and Bradshaw weakened the attack. The chief stumbling block to success was Baxter, the home custodian, who, as usual, distinguished himself against Warrington. He made more openings for his threequarters than his own half-backs—an eolquent testimony to the quality of his full-back play.

I took the opportunity on Saturday of watching the juniors whom Warrington had out on trial against Rochdale "A" at Wilderspool. The team as a whole played a shocking game and was well beaten. Chester and Vernon might never have seen a Rugby match before judging by their poverty of ideas in half .back play. And at centre young Butterworth waa no better. The redeetning feature of the display was the work of the new fullback, Jolley, who shaped like a footballer who will make his name in the game. To my mind he is too small to ever be a great success at full-back, but there is talent in the lad which might find a more suitable outlet at half-back.

At Dublin. COUNTY CHAMPIONSITIP—FusrAL. Devon

' At Devonport. Warrington Fight Hard at Rochdale. Warrington made a good show at Rochdale on Satur- day, considering that Brooks and nradshaw were absent from the back division, but they had to submit to defeat in the end. Fine weather prevailed, and a crowd of 6,000 watched, what was always an interesting struggle. The teams were : Warrington—Mclntyre back; Woodburn, Renwick, Dickenson and Clare, threequarter backs; Daintith and Jordan, halfbacks; Thomas, Shugars, Chesters, Fearnley Tranter and Skelhorne, forwards. Rochdale Hornets—Baxter, back ; West, English, Prudence end Paddon, threequarter backs ; Schofield and Robinaon, half backs ; Woods, Homan, Turner, Slade, Jenkins and Fitzsimmons, forwards. Referee, Mr J C Lnmhy, Leeds. Touch-judges, Messrs W A Peel and A Widdison. Warrington opened well, for the forwards put in some dashing work, and Baxter was hard put to it to save his line. Renwick and Dickenson made a good use of the many passes heaved out from the scrum, but the wings were unable to beat the defence. Clare made one gallant effort, and it took Baxter all his time to keep the Warrington lad from crossing. Schofield broke away smartly, and cleared finely, but Daintith soon placed Warrington on the offensive again, West being tackled near the line by the speedy Skelhorne. The Rochdale forwards at last found their feet, and Romans led a dribble to the Warrington quarter. There was some loose play here, and the ball coming to Baxter, the full back opened the score with a fine drop-kick. For a time Mclntyre was busy but be cleared strongly, and fine work by

Renwick and Clare put Warrington again on the attack The Hornets were penned in, and Woodburn twice bad a shot at goal, but failed by inches. Mclntyre also failed and a goal kicker seemed badly needed. Rochdale cleared the way to the Warrington end through Robinson and English, Paddon taking a pass and making a good bid for the corner, where Mclntyre was too much for him. Warrington still had the better of matters, the halves, Renwick and Skelliorne, all being prominent in another attack on the home line. Shugars burst through and gave Woodburn a fine chance, but the wing man failed to side-step and fell a victim to Baxier's fine tackling. Warrington ought to have been ahead by now, but the interval came with the home team leading by a goal to nil. On resuming, for a long time the play was of an even nature. Warrington eventually forced matters to such purpose that Baxter was lucky to clear. Daintith threw to Thomas, who made a desperate effort to cross but he was grassed by Slade. By easy stages the Hornets forced matters at the opposite end and a passing move- ment by Schofield, Robinson and English looked ominous for the visitors, but Paddon failed to break through. Warrington were penalized, but nothing accrued. The visitors were next awarded a penalty,but Mclntyre was lacking in length in the attempt at goal. The game was warmly contested at the Rochdale end, until the home custodian initiated a round of passing and the ball travelled through the hands of English, Prudence and Paddon. Danger was averted, but not for long, for another round of passing led the way for Roman to snap up the ball and send Turner over for a try. Paddon failed to improve. This success practically settled the issue of the game, for Warrington appeared to fall away and were usually employed on the defence. Paddon scored a try in the closing stages, but the goal. kick was again a failure. Result: Rochdale Hornets, one goal two tries (eight points) ; Warrington, nil. WIDNES v. BARROW.

- - At Widnes before 2,000 spectators, bath teams being well represented. Early on Swinton got over for Widnes, and Reid and Johnson crossed the line, Jones (and Taylor gosling. Mitchell and Gifford scored for Barrow. In the second half tries were added for Widnes by Swinton, Taylor and Reid, while Jones (two) and Taylor smiled. Result :—Widnes 5 goals 6 tries 28 points ), Barrow 2 tries (6 points). ST. HELENS' 0 REAT WIN AT LEIGH.

ROYAL COURT THEATRE.

"A Life's Revenge."

The popularity of the orthodox melodrama is declining in face of the rapid advance of lighter fare and a play has to possess exceptionally good qualities to gain the appreciation of theatre patrons of to-day. However, notwithstanding the long term of years" A Life's Revenge " has been staged, its appearance is always heartily welcomed for it is one of those few productions which possess in a marked degree the essentials to a successful drama. Overflowing with exciting incidents, the balance is maintained by a good fund of rich humour. The plot is cleverly con- ceived. A serf-painter falls in love with one of his patrons—a ,princess, who is also loved by the most notorious and most cruel prince in Russia. Her preference however, is for the poor painter and they are secretly married,- a fact which is discovered by an adventuress who herself has amorous designs upon the artist and resorts to various measures to obtain her desire. A realistic duel is witnessed between the rivals and, needless to say, the honours fall to the heroine. By the laws of Russia a serf is under the entire control of his master who can use him and punish him as he likes. The prince succeeds in getting the painter in his power and suLjects him to the most degrading treatment. A rescue 18 affected by English friends of the artist and after many incidents of a thrilling character have been witnessed, the villian receives his just deserts. Mr. and Mrs. Percy Brown have presented the play with a large amount of success at the Royal Court Theatre during the week. They are well supported by a number of talented actors and actresses. The difficult part of Paul Starovitch, the painter, is in the able bands of Mr Reginald Brown who possesses an attractive personality and is a great favourite. As his wife, Princess Wanda Ivyala, Miss Kathleen Magee soon instals herself in the good esteem of the audiences, her acting being superb. Mr. Percy Brown, as Prince Boris Strogroff makes the most of a trying and unpopular part. Of herculean build, he contrives by a mastery of facial expression and caustic speech, to show all the cunning and devilry of a thorough-paced scoundrel and earns his quota of hisses- the villain's highest reward. Miss Lelia Drummond as the Red Angel, Marcia Victoria, gives a convincing portrayal of the scorned and jealous woman. In the duel scene she shows herself to be a no mean exponent of the art of fencing, and along with the Princess earns unstinted applause. Mr. Cyril Grier. as the wily serf Michael Orloff, ranks amongst the foremost of the company, and justly so, for his part is a curiously contradictory one and one the audience do not know whether to appreciate or scorn until the final scene when be shows himself in his true light. The other parts are in capable bands and the scenery and effects are perfect. The orchestra, under Mr. Tom Booth- royd's conductorship, submits a choice programme.

NEXT WEEK'S PROGRAMME. Mrs. F. C. Kimberley's latest play, " The Wild Girl of the Forest," will be presented at the Royal Court Theatre next week. As the title suggests, it is quite different from the usual run of drama. The central idea came to the author in a dream, and the story is suitably pitched in a key of high romance. It is by no means an uncommon experience for creative artistes to have an inspiration when the mind is supposed to be at rest. In this instance, the situation in which Mrs. Kimberley seems to find herself appealed to her so strongly that she set to work at once to weave it into a play. The drama was success- fully produced in Manchester,on August Bank Holiday. 1911, and it has proved, by far. Mrs. Kimberley's biggest success. The caste includes Algernon T. Hicks, as loris Prince of Floralia ; George E. Butler, as Capt. Riccardo, the traitor; and Miss Margaret From°, as Maritza, the Wild Girt.

ALBERT HALL SUFFRAGE MEETING.

The Albert Hall was packed on the 23rd, when the National Union of Women's Suffrage Societies held its great meeting. The hall was decorated with the banners of its different branches (over 300 in number), and the lady stewards wore red, white and green sashes and favours.

Mrs. Fawcett, L.L.D., (President of the Union) took the chair, and the speakers were Mrs. Philip Snowdeo, Lord Lytton and Mr. Lloyd George.—The latter came in for some heckling from a few of the militant suffragettes, who profess to disbelieve the Govern- ment's sincerity in promising facilities for a Women's Suffrage Amendment to the Reform Bill but the Chancellor of the Exchequer made it quite clear that the Cabinet would stand by the pledges, and affirmed his belief that this year would see the enfranchisement of women. A host of distinguished people had accepted platform tickets for the occasion. Literature being I particularly well represented in the persons of Mr John (3ataworthy, Mr Jerome K Jerome and Mr Pett Ridge. Other notables included Miss Ellen Terry, Professor I Gilbert Murray the lion. John Collier and Mrs Kendall. A collection was taken during the course of the even- ing and resulted in an addition of £5,000 to the Society's fends. Apparently it is not only the Militant Suffragettes who can rake in the shekels !

bad always been that there was not enough money in the compensation funds. The compensation levy was a most unsatisfactory thing, for it was optional to the local authorities to impose it and he believed some authorities had never imposed it. The provision for local option eas real democracy—one ratepayer, one vote. The Tory party should hail it with delight as a kind of referendum. (Laughter.) He felt strongly that there should be greater stringency with regard to clubs. The alleged aims of the clubs should be minutely investigated with a view to stamping out drinking dens. Grocers' licences should be abolished, for they put temptation directly in the way of the weak and placed a premium upon deceit. It had been said that men could not be made saber by Act of Parlia- ment, but legislation could make it easier for men to do right and harder for them to do wrong. The PRlxte MilcisTEß (Mr. E. Wynne-Jones) said drunkenness was Britain's most conspicuous vice. It was a standing disgrace to the nation that we planted legalised dangers at every street corner—especially in the places where the people were the least able to get away from them. The Bill was not extreme, but was based on the report of Lord Peel's Commission which included eight representatives of " the trade." As to the suggested municipalization of the drink traffic, they could not do that, because they had the authority of Alderman Bennett for saying that some of their present local rulers wero no more fit to govern the town than a donkey was to sing a Christmas carol. (Laughter.) Municipalization was therefore out of the question. Local option was the best solution of the problem at the present time, because it would awaken the moral sense of the people, and get them to expel the evil from their midst. By reducing the facilities for drinking they lessened the consumption of drink. As to the complaint against restrictions, if people would not do right, they must make them. Mr. WILSON (C., Hythe), contended that the theory that a reduction of licences meant a decrease of drunkenness was not borne out by experience in different parts of the country. A recent return showed that on the average the counties and boroughs with the smallest number of public houses per 10.000 of the population had recorded the largest percentage of con- victions for drunkenness pro rata. They all admitted that licences were granted for one year but public- houses were purchased on the understanding that renewal would take place annually so long as tbe licencee behaved himself according to the law. ("No.") It would therefore be unjust to take away licences without compensation. Mr. LEsLIE (L., Bury) gave an unqualified approval to the Bill because it was a step towards an ideal which would end in the abolition of the drink trade. Local option was simply a restriction of sale and not of purchase. A man in a prohibited area could get drink if he wanted it, outside that area. He regretted that a party with the great traditions of the Conservative party—however remote those traditions might be— should associate itself, as it did, with the drink trade. Public opinion was rapidly advancing in favour of reform. It was ready for a much greater reduction of licences but the justices were at present hampered by the Act of 1904, which was passed at the ultimatum of the brewers. The vested interest which that Act created must not be regarded as a freehold and the present Bill would win back for the magistrates the rights which they possessed prior to 1904. The Government did not object to "the trade' providing compensation, hut they said that the taking away of licences must not depend upon the amount of compensation which the brewers had a mind to exact from their own members. The magistrates must have unfettered rights to reduce licences apart from any considerations of fioance. The progressive forces were fighting for righteous land laws, better housing and improved social conditions for the poor, but the drink trade was a barrier in the way of all pro- gress. It was the Judas of democracy. Mr. Ceameenr.are (Soc. Gorton) expressed surprise at the mild way in which the Government was dealing with the drink traffic. He opposed the Bill because it was not conducive to the best interests of the people at large. They could take a horse to water but they could not make him drink. They were not going to make a sober nation by coercion. It was not drink which caused the poverty in this country and reduced I men to the verge of starvation. It was the conditions under which they laboured and the small wages they received. The Bill would deprive the people of that freedom which was supposed to be one of the greatest factors in our laws. Seeing that the community created the value of a licence, why was it allowed to go to the brewer? The State must take over the control of the drink traffic instead of leaving it in the bands of privi- leged persons whose livelihood or existence depended upon the misery of the poor. Mr. J. Mr:Dee:ern (C., Paisley) said they had been treated to advocacy of temperance by most intemperate language. (Laughter,) The Bill, he considered, would aggravate and accentuate existing grievances. Power would be vested in local magistrates, many of whom allowed an utter incapacity to deal with the question, through prejudice and incompetence. The Libeials were continually tinkering with the licensing question, but without any advantage accruing—in fact it was almost a hardy annual, The fullest and freest local option was that which allowed the individual freedom to take or leave drink. If 'they:wished to make reliable pro- gress in the propagation of temperance, they must operate on the individual and his inclinational? the Bill became law, the Bev. H. Ogden's health would be heartily drunk from the Tweed to John-o'-Groats, because it would have the effect of opening public- houses in Scotland on Sunday. (Laughter.) The speaker reminded the Government that the grocers' licences which had been roundly condemned, were the creation of the Liberal party, and ,when they bad been in power they had not abolished them. Total prohibition of the drink trade would be a distinct injustice not only to those who were engaged in it but to the general public. The Rev. H. OGDEN, in replying to the debate, said he was most amazed at the attitude of the Socialists. The Bill ought certainly to have appealed to them on the ground of trusting the people. On a division, the Bill was passed by 21 votes to 11. WELSH DISESTABLISHMENT. Next Tuesday evening the following resolution will be introduced by the Prime Minister and the Chancellor of the Exchequer: That this House welcomes the statement of Mr. Asquith " That a Bill for the Dis- establishment and Disendowment of the Church of England in Wales shall be given such a position in the programme of legislation for 3912, as will enable it to overide the Veto of the House of Lords during the present Parliament." This House fully recognises that the withdravtal of the patronage and control of the State from the Episcopal Church is absolutely necessary for the social harmony of the people and further expresses the opinion that in the interests of justice religious equality should be granted to Wales. This House further affirms that after an equitable satisfaction of existing interests the national property now devoted to the use of the Churoh shall be devoted to national purposes.

COMMERCIAL FALLURES.—According to "Kemp' Mercantile Gazette," the number of Commercial Failures published in Great Britain and Ireland during the four weeks ending Saturday, February- 24th,was 649. The number in the corresponding four weeks of last year was 736, showing a decrease of 87, being a net decrease in 1912, to date, of 87.

LOCAL PARLIAMENT.

LICENSING BILL PASSED.

After RD interesting and spirited debate, the Licensing Bill introduced by the Rev H Ogden (Minister for Education) received approval at the meeting of the Debating Society on Tuesday evening by a majority of 10 votes. Some novel points were raised in the course of the discussion—which was extended about twenty minutes beyond the usual time—and the feature which most surpised the intro- ducer of the measure was the opposition which came from the Socialists. Their remedy for existing evils RIM of course. State ownership. The CHANCELLOR OF THE EXCFIEQUER (Councillor Hawthorn), in re-opening the debate, said the Bill might be regarded as one of restitution, for it restored to the magistrates the right which was taken away from them by the Licensing Act of 1904. Prior to that date, the law was perfectly clear that licences were granted for twelve months only. Although the granting of compensation had not altogether stopped the extinction of licences, it had retarded progress in that direction, with the result that we had many more licences than were required to meet the reasonable wants of the community. The Rector of Warrington once said that one licence for every 1.000 inhabitants of Warrington would be sufficient. That 'seemed a reasonable proposition but we were a long way from attaining it,as there was one licensed house for about every 300 inhabitants.By establishing local option the Bill gave the inhabitsnts of any district the right to say whether they would have a licensed house in their midst or not. It was common knowledge that the opening of a public-house reduced the value of pro- perty in the locality. Poor people had not the power and money to fight brewers' applications and conse- quently licensed houses had been thrust at almost every street corner in the poorer neighbourhoods and had become a source of temptation to those who ought to be protected by law from such inflictions. It was amongst the poorest class of workers that the greatest drunkenness prevailed. ("Question.") Two hundred cases which recently came before the Liverpool justices were analysed and it was found that 80 per cent concerned people in receipt of less than 30s. per week. Defending the proposal to curtail the hours of business at public-houses, Councillor Hawthorn said it was utterly antagonistic to democracy that any class of people should be required to labour from 6 a.m. to 11 p.m. If there was a hard worked class of the community it was the barman and the barmaid. The hardest taskmaster in the world was sometimes tbe workingman who demanded an eight hours' day for himself and asked barmen and shopmen to work ten or fourteen hours per day, because he would not do his

purchasing earlier. The Bill, he finally claimed, dealt with the evil in a moderate manner and would tend to make England more sober. Mr. L. WARBURTON (C., W. Bristol), said the Con- 1 servatives would offer the 13111 a strenuous and deter- mined Opposition. How a measure which failed in every element of justice could be regared as generous, he failed to see. The magistrates, he contended, bad the same power to-day as they bad previous to the 1904 Act. ("No, no.") Nominally licences were granted for a year but would any sensible man go to the expense of erecting a public-house and establishing a business on the understanding that he might be turned out at the end of twelve months? The Government omitted to mention that " the trade" itself raised the compensation under the 1904 Act. He considered it was only fair that if the public demanded the suppression of a licence, a reasonable amount of compensation should be paid for It. He agreed with the proposed restrictions on clubs, which, bethought, competed unfairly with the publican and were often responsible for (drunkenness for which he was wrongly blamed. With regard to the Bill as a whole, he advised the Government to teach the people self-control instead of hedging them round with all sorts of fences to keep them out of the public-houses. Were the Liberal party, he asked, willing to find posts for the men they threw out of employment by their legislation, instead of providing appointments for faithful adherents of Liberalism ? . _ _

I Mr. E. ENGLAND (1., Warrington) after congratulating the Rev. H. Ogden on the very temperate language he used in introducing the Bill, said he was a whole- hearted supporter of the measure. He considered it was very just and would in many respects be a great help to the publican. If any man needed help from the public, it was the publican. By calling upon him to work such long hours they were inflicting a hardship upon him. The Bill was not ideal by any means. Mr. Balfour's Act with regard to compensation was a statesmanlike Act. The method for providing com- pensation on the extinction of licences was excellently devised and operated well. He was surprised at the Opposition opposing the present measure, instead of suggesting amendments, for he believed every scheme ought to be considered on its merits, apart from party prejudice. Ile was particularly pleased that the War- rington magistrates had supported the recommendations of the Liverpool bench on licensing reform because, unfortunately, a slur had been cast on them in days gone by. It had been said more than once that the Warrington justices were in the hands of the brewers, but their recent action gave the lie direct to that allegation. They had shown that they were an impartial body. Mr. WATSON (Soc., E. Birmingham) contended that the cure for the evils of the drink traffic was public o wnership of breweries and public-houses. The breweries had, however, so accustomed the people to chemicalised water that if a perfect beer were supplied the novelty might upset them. (Laughter.). They ought to close the low, dirty back-street public-houses and re-plant them in the open sunshine, making them bright and wholesome places. He would put two or three in Palmyra-square. (Laughter.) Let them make the public-house a place where a man could, with comfort, take his wife and family to. find music and light enter- tainment. At present magistrates were appointed on account of political bias and not for their knowledge. They ought to be elected, like members of the Cotincil, and then the public would have more control over them. If the Government were prepared to allow the work- ingclasses to be dragged up amid slums and filth they could only expect them to resort to beer to wash down their sufferings and sins. Mr. W. .J. WALKER (I, Bermondsey) declared himself to be "one of those terrible persons known as rabid teetotallers." If all classes could be compelled to adopt teetotalism they would be the better for it and a large percentage of our social evils would disappear. By the ultimate abolition of compensation the Bill would remove the greatest hindrance to the reduction of licences. The Act of 1904 had not realized the expectations of its authors in that respect, for the cry

TUE EXAMINER.

SATURDAY MARCH 2 1912.

-,_ Thb There have been few ' btADLOCK. occasions within living memory when the beat,. no: of the national pulse gave such evid- dats,43 °f a common anxiety as in the last few th's• There had been a general expectation -tratiat When. the Government took ltihe coal viin,e situation in hand there wold be a / ~ is 13" C decision in one direction or another. The Examiner, Saturday, IY[arch, 2, 1912.

===== 0002601_19120302_art0051.txt | included=true | included =====
be worth the closing of the mines for even so much as a week. But it is not only the increased cost of output that has made coal- owners, particularly those of South Wales, seem to be harshly resolute in resistance. They have had many troubles to contend with in late years, and there cannot, we believe, be a doubt that they were eager to crush the men, and that this desire has pre- vailed over all other considerations. If they could have shown that crushing the men must be a good po!icy in the long run, there , would at least be justification for their stubborness—or their firmness. however it may be named from their own point of view.

The first announcement that was made as to the result of the deliberations of Wednesday put one thing quite clearly, and that is that the coalowners were not agreed among themselves. There seems to be no reason to doubt that the negotiations might have been brought to a speedy end in one way or another if the coalowners had been as much of one mind as the men whom they employ. But different conditions prevail all over the coalfield, and not only different con- ditions, but very great differences of temper. The coalowners are far indeed from thinking alike. It is natural to say that each of them knows his lowa business best. But in that case, why are there great federations of employers? It is just as with the coal pits themselves. Some of them yield very great profits, and some of them are scarcely worth the working. Common action among coal- owners must in such circumstances be exceptionally difficult, and the impossibility of bringing all the interests concerned to a common denominator, as it were, has made the Government's task of conciliation one of the most difficult and bewildering undertak- ings in which any Government has ever engaged. The whole country must feel that the proposals made by the Government are the real and only possible foundations for a settlement. They will not, as appears, pre- vent a strike; but what seems almost certain is that they will greatly shorten its duration.

Mr. W. H. Woodcock, THE RATEABLE the able assistant over- VALUE OF seer, has now completed THE BOROUGH. the revaluation of the Borough, as authorised last October, and presented his report to the Finance Committee. The work has been very carefully done, and the results give a clearer idea of the "letting value" of the town than has ever been ascertained before. Our rate- able value to-day is £294,184 10s., as against £257,967 six years ago, an increase of 14 per cent. An extra productiveness for rating purposes of £1,016 per annum, is estimated. Mr. Woodcock comments in favourable terms on the housing of the workingclasses, and his opinion is of exceptional value seeing that during the last four months the rentals (and the condition also) of over 15,000 houses and shops have been ascertained. To quote Mr. Woodcock's own words, "The working- classes particularly are well-catered for, and for a reasonable rent are able to obtain a well-built and comfortable house. The slums are gradually disappearing, they were mostly in yards and courts, and soon I hope the last of these will disappear." In a way, the over- seer's comments are an unsolicited testimonial to the energetic policy of the Health Com- mittee in dealing with congested, or slum areas. A great advance towards healthy and adequate housing has been made in the past few years and there is no cause to doubt that in a short time slums in Warrington will be as rare as once they were plentiful. It is very gratifying to find that with the steady increase in rateable value,there is a coincident improvement in the conditions of living among the working classes and even the poorest of the poor.

The Member for War- MERELY A MILLION rington rarely fails to OR Two. _attract attention when

he makes a speech in public, for the simple reason that he utters more mistatements to the minute than any contemporary politician we can call to mind at the moment. The natural sequence is that Liberals are fully employed in putting him right." In the House of Commons last week Mr. Smith made the assertion that our increased trade was largely with our Colonies Quite the opposite is the actual fact, as Councillor Roberts took the trouble to explain the other evening. The total average increase in our exports to protected countries during the five years, 1906-10, was 39'3 per cent., and to open markets 452 per Ant. while the increase in exports to Colonial markets, notwithstanding our boasted preference, was only 142 per cent. " baying been arranged to take place between the Council and the members, Mr T E Knowles led off in the affirmative on behalf of the Members, being supported by Messrs Forshaw, Dutton and Elliott, and Was followed by Mr J Walsh in the negative on behalf of the Council, supported by Messrs E G Burgess. Warrington Photographic Society. For the third time this se.sion Mr. Bishop has increased the society's indeptedness to him, this time by a demonstration of the working in Platinotype Paper. That he knows his subject is proved by any one who will turn over his old pages of the Amateur Photographer, and find the reproduction (Summer Number 1910) of Mr. Bishop's fine platinotype of "Roses." After a succint reference to the cheapness and the simplicity of its manipulation, he demon- strated to the satisfaction of the audience what beautiful results can be obtained either in black and white or in sepia by this process. The ten prints enclosed by the Platiootype Company included both portraits and landscapes, and every gradation of tone was obtained as each print was developed. One p tint .dVout this particular brand of paper was not explained and it is a mystery to the- ordinary Englishman. Although the price of metallic platinum has so greatly increased, probably through the multiplication of electrical apparatus, yet neither in quality nor in price has platinotype paper varied for a dozen or more years. • Threatened Strike of Bakers The operative bakers and confectioners of Warrington are agitating for a reduction in the hours and an increase of wages. Negotiations with the Master Bakers' Association have been pending for the past few weeks and recently the Amalgamated Union of Operative Bakers and confectioners formulated their demands which include a 51 hours week (including meal times), a minimum wage of 38s. for foremen; 325. for second bands, and 30s. far all adult hands, and various provisions as to overtime, &c. It was understood that if the men were not satisfied they intended to come out on strike to-day (SAurday). but last night we were informed that a further meeting between representatives of the masters and employees had been arranged for Tuesday next. 11.10 TOR CYCLES.—Agent for "Douglas "& "Premier." NOTES AND NO'llitE

An echo of the snowstorm! The Borough Surveyor reports that after the recent storm his department removed 2308 loads of snow weighing 1731 tons, at a cost of £221. 19s. 11d.

* - "Wales is so busy digging underground that her children are forgetting how to live ' decently above ground."—From Thoughts for St. David's Day" by Alfred T. Davies. * *

Howley foot-bridge has been ready for delivery by the contractors for some time, All that is required is a little more haste on the part of the Corporation in making the foundations. The piles are not driven yet, but there is a reasonable hope that the bridge may be ready for the summer.

Has the Health Committee already got weary of well doing? We notice that the Committee has resolved not to be represented at the Exhibition and Conference of the Smoke Abatement League in London next month. Alderman Bennett seems to have a clear cases belli against his confreres.

to The Lancashire ana Cheshire Federation of the National League of Young Liberals this week completes its fourth year, and the -report for the past twelve months shows that 1911 saw a still more marked advance in its activities and achievements. During the year nine new branches have been affiliated with the Federation. * * Mr. Harold. Smith, M.P., fell foul of the Irishmen in the House on Wednesday during a discussion on the effects of the rail- way strike in Dublin. He admitted being there himself "on a holiday" and was twitted by Mr. Jos. Devlin, M.P. with going" over to Dublin with fifty-five boxes of pamphlets" and bearing a placard on his breast saying that" Ulster will fight and Liverpool will be there to lead." Mr. Smith neither denied nor confirmed this interesting suggestion that he filled the role of a sandwichman. We are sure his constituents would like to know fuller details in order to stimulate their imagination.

* * Mr. Smith seems to have been caught in the act, so to speak, of adopting devious means to prejudice Home Rule. Our readers will recollect that not very long ago he said publicly. If they thrust upon Ulster a measure of Home Rule Londonderry would run blood, and the con- stituents of his native city would cross the channel by thousands to take up arms etc. Perhaps by " arms " Mr. Smith meant placards and pamphlets. Mr. Devlin hopes to be there when the member for Warrington enters his " sphere of warlike activity in Ireland. " In the meantime he told the House that as an Irishman,he much resented "attacks upon Ireland by a number of ignorant English gentlemen of high education and great prestige. " *s* "In the United States of North America the slavery of Protection is to-day immeasur- ably more crushing, more soul-warping and demoralising over the whites than was the slavery of the blacks the cant-inspired,loath- some Civil War was entered upon professedly to abolish. Protectionism is now sterilising France, leading her down towards the abyss of new revolutions, and in Germany it is sapping the foundations of the Empire, hastening on the day when the German people will have to choose between hopeless and abject vassaldom and revolt that may give a chance to recover freedom—between Kaiserism and individual manhood."—" The Investors' Review," Feb. 17th, 1912.

* * For his life of Lord Randolph Churchill,the publishers paid Mr. Winston Churchill £B,OOO, a higher rate of payment than that made to Lord Morley for his great Gladstone biography. For this Mr. John Morley, as he then was, received £lO,OOO, but the book was nearly a third larger than Mr. Churchill's, and took him a good two years to write. The Gladstone family, who commenced the work, are said to have already received £36,000 from its sale, in addition to which the profits of the publishers have to be added. Mr. Kipling's book, 'Captain Courageous," was written for a magazine at a shilling a word, then a record, but since often exceeded. Sir A. Conan Doyle has written stories which , have brought him in half-a-crown a word.

I. afoul

* * A little plain speaking sometimes clears the air, but we doubt if the "Sunrise" say- ings will have this effect. We quoted from the local annual last week, but we did not by any means exhaust the mine of invective. A reference to the work of local architects as exemplified in some of our public buildings reads: in their pathetic striving after originality they have perpetrated such abominations as to make everybody with a spark of taste feel positively ill." The writer also says that the projected scheme for the enlargement of the Town Hall "is enough to make the ghosts of the Wilson-Pattens turn in their graves." This, at least, will be a new feat for ghosts, which are usually understood to be quite independent of the grave's close environment. * • But this is not all, or even the worst ! There are some caustic allusions to our "municipal rulers" and the usefulness of expecting from them "any glimmering of genuine statesmanship." We are told that "our hope is in the children," and led to anticipate nothing of value from" municipal administrators some of whom are no more fit to manage the town's affairs than a donkey is to sing a Christmas carol." This trenchant article ends on the following cheerful note:

"There's a good time coming, boys, Wait a little longer."

We have a mental picture of some of the " boys " attending the Council meeting on Tuesday armed with copies of "The Sunrise Annual." There is obviously " a good time coming "for a certain Alderman! , •-

In the Divorce Court, on Monday, Sir Samuel Evans, the President, beaid the undefended suit of Mrs. Gertrude Edith Sackson, of Waterloo House, Wilderspol, Warrington, for a divorce from her husband, Robert Holmes Jackson, on the ground of his misconduct and cruelty.—Mr. Bayford, for the petitioner, stated that the parties were married on the 10th October, 1909. In June, 1911, petitioner found in her husband's room a diary which contained entries in shorthand. It was, he thought, the most disgusting document he had ever read. The entries had been translated. When her husband cameho wanted to get the document from her, but she declined. Next day he left the house, and they had not lived together since. Soon afterwards plaintiff consulted a doct and found she was suffering in health, a conditio. she attributed directly to her husband.—Tr. Law gave evidence in support of the wife's statement. His Lordship granted a decree nisi, with costs. A Camera Tour. The Parkinson family, of Barton-on-Irwell, paid another welcome visit to Bold-street Wesleyan School on Saturday evening, and gave their popular enter- tainment, "Our tour with the camera to Maderia, Teneriffe and Grand Canary," interspersed with musical items. The Rev. H. Williamson presided. The lecture proved highly interesting and the lime- light views,which numbered over 190, were reproduced from choice photographs of the many beauty spots in the places visited. Miss Olive Parkinson gave expressive renderings of " The brightest day" and ".Nearer, my God, to Thee," and Miss Parkinson skilfully played a violin solo—selection from "Faust." Mr. H. Parkinson gave as a pianoforte silo Thalberg's " Home sweet home," and a trio was sung by Miss and Messrs. T. M. and H. Parkinson, while a number of selections were given in which all the members of the family took part. The entertainment closed with the singing of " Home, sweet home," by Miss Parkinson.
