MR. HAROLD- SMITH, M.P. ON "HOLIDAY." ' A MISSION TO DUBLIN. DID HE CARRY A PLACARD?

In the House of Commons on Wednesday. Mr. Harold Smith, M P., intervened in a debate on the Irish Railway Strike. MR. HAROLD SMITH'S ADVENTURES. Mr. H. Smith (Warrington, C.), said he could not accept the statement of the Chief Secretary that adequate protection was given to traders in Dublin during the strike. Ile himself was in Dublin for some time during that period, and endeavoured to get some goods forwarded to friends who lived about thirty- five miles out of Dublin. He went down to the quay to get the goods out of one of the Tedcastle steamers, and interviewed no fewer than three different firms of carters in an endeavour to persuade them to do their legitimate transporting to the railway station. There was not one of them who would do this,with the result that the only remedy was to order a motor- lurry to take the goods the thirty-five miles, thus depriving a charitable object of the money this motor carriage cost. In view of that experience, what nonsense it was for hon. members who knew nothing of the circumstances to say there was no trouble, and that anyone who wanted could carry on their ordinary business.

Mr. Birrell : Did these people apply for police protection ? Mr. Smith said they did not apply because they had applied, and had not received sufficient protection for their carters, horses, and property. The right hon. gentleman wisely refrained from telling the house during what part of the period of the strike be was in Dublin. He (Mr. Smith) was there for two weeks, and every morning the Irish papers were blazing the fact that the Chief Secretary was not there. There was no doubt that during the strike citizens of Dublin were deprived of their elementary rights, and for the Chief Secretary to be absent was a disgraceful thing, very discreditable to the right hon. gentleman. Mr. Devlin (W. Belfast, Nat.) said it was obvious that some hon. gentlemen went to Dublin during the strike to make trouble. What was the bon member for Warrington doing there at that time ? Mr. Smith : I was on a holiday. Mr. Devlin said that the hon. member went over to Dublin with fifty-five boxes of Unionist pamplets. (Laughter.) He did not tell them that he had a placard on his breast saying that " Ulster will fight and Liverpool will be there to lead." (Great laughter.) The Chairman said that the hon. member was again opening up a controversy which was not desirable to pursue. Mr. Devlin said he would not proceed with it beyond remarking that he supposed the hon. member ' would later on proceed to a sphere of warlike activity in Ireland. (Laughter.) When that time came they would all be supposed to be there to see. He would say there was no disorder in Dublin. As an Irishman, he (Mr. Devlin) much resented attacks upon Ireland by a number of ignorant English gentlemen of high education and great prestige. (Nationalist laughter). After some further discussion, Captain Craig moved to report progress as a pro- test against the absence of any representative of the Treasury. The motion was defeated by 237 to 127. The vote was agreed to.

COUNCILLOR ROBERTS AND TORY ATTACKS.

WARRINGTON AND IMPORTED WIRE.

SPIRITED REPLY TO MR. HAROLD SMITH, M.P. Speaking at a Liberal meeting in Whitecross Ward on Wednesday evening, Councillor Henry Roberts said that was the first opportunity he had had since he made a little speech a fortnight ago with regard to the question of Free Trade, to reply to an atte-npt made by some responsible or irresponsible pertons connected with the Conservative Club to hold him up to ridicule and, he supposed, to contempt. He was accused of having made a silly assertion. (Laughter) He was not going to say he never did make a silly assertion—(laughter)—but what he did say was, that in regard to the important question of Free Trade or any other important question Affecting the interests and well-being of the community at large, be did not plead guilty to ever having made a silly assertion. (Applause). What was the assertion referred to? Speaking at Stockton heath a fortnight ago, be referred to that exhibition, called a

DUMPING SIIOIV, which was held in Warrington a few weeks ago—an exhibition which had done more to create in the minds of thoughtfill workingmen a determination to have nothing to do with tariff reform than all the efforts that the Free Traders of Warrington had put forth for a considerable period. (Applause.) He was referring tl the fAct that amongst other things shown at the dumping show was a specimen of German wire and he made a statement that the quantity of German wire that came into Warrington could be put into any one of his audience's eyes. "I did not think it necessary," continued Councillor Roberts, "to explain to a meeting a intelligent Liberals, largely composed of workingmen, that I was using what is called a. figure of speech. I did not intend anybody to under- stand literally that they could take a piece of wire, as representing the total product of German wire that came into Warrington, and put it in their eyes. (Laughter.) I beg to repeat that I was speaking to a meeting of intelligent Liberals. John Bright once said that the

===== 0002601_19120302_art0055.txt | included=true | included =====
TORY PARTY WAS TITE STUPID PARTY

but I certainly did not expect to find such a remark- able illustration of the truth of that definition as applied to the present Tory party, as that any Tory would be such an ass as to have taken literally what I said. (Laughter.) What I meant and what I stand by is this : that the quantity of German wire coining into Warrington is a comparatively small and negligible quantity—in other words, that it is not worth taking the slightest notice of. Then they go on to make an assertion which I won't describe as silly, because it carries its own contradiction. If it is intended as a reply to my statement, it is what Mr. Churchill would call a terminological inexactitude, and what another prominent man years ago called a big, thumping lie. (Laughter.) I will tell you the statement and leave you to form your own opinion as to its exactitude or otherwise. They go on to say that .£1,032,067 worth of foreign made wire, wire nails,rolls (I think it should have been rods) and screws were damped here in 1910. ' Dumped here.' I was talking about German wire coming into War- rington. If they say £1,632,667 worth of foreign-made wire nails, rods and screws were dumped in Warring- ton in 1910, my vocabulary is absolutely unequal to describe the EXTRAVAGANCE OF THE 14ALSEITOOD

which was conveyed in those words.Wiiether they meant to say that or not, they meant the people of Warrington who do not understand the facts and figures, to understand that that was the quantity of material dumped in Wariington, because it was Warrington we were speaking about This is the kind of thing that is offered to intelligent politicians in this town on behalf of the programme of tariff reform. What is the fact? The whole import of foreign wire, German and other- wise, that came into the country—not into Warrington —in 1911, the whole of it, lock, stock and barrel was only £440,000 worth. These are the Board of Trade returns and yet they have the audacity to put a placard out desclibing my statement—which I forgive them, for of course, their ignorance is an explanation of it—as a silly assertion. Continuing, Councillor ROBERTS pointed out that in BSS there was a very strong feeling in this town about the

IMPORTATION OF GERMAN WIRE. At that time a great deal of German wire—fifties and hundreds of tons at a time—was coming into Warring.. ton and was being dealt with here. It was largely bought for the purpose of the manufacture of wire netting. It was a very sore question. However,mastere and men get together and considered the situation. There was no question of "That is our limit " on the part of either masters or men, both haying realized that something had to done if the British trade in the manufacture of wire was to be retained and improved. The men were wisely led, the masters were moderate in their propositions, and the result was that a new scale of wages was arranged and the question as to what kind of machinery should be introduced was agreed to. What bad been the result? One of the finest works in this country—a monument to the ability.and com- mercial enterprise cf those at the head of it, a monu- ment equally to the loyalty, thrift and workmanship of the men employed in it, and A MONUMENT TO THE PRINCIPLES OF FREE TRADE. They all knew the concern he hau in mina, ace it add grown by leaps and bounds and to-day it was not necessary—because they could produce the same wire that they required for all their purposes far cheaper than they could buy it in Germany—to introduce any German wire. Not only bad they again captured the British tasrket, but they had positively knocked the German out in Australia, for the wire he used to send here in such large quantities. (Applause.) Councillor Roberts then alluded to the recent speech of their respected Member, which, he said, had been SO MUCH FLAUNTED AND ADVERTISED BY CAREFUL

EXTRACTS from Conservative newspapers as being such a striking and trenchant speech on the question of tariff reform. He would call their attention to three points and then leave them to form their opinion as to the value of the statements of the Member unon commercial questions in regard to which he (Councillor Roberts) ventured to suggest he was profoundly ignorant. Referring to the boom in trade, Mr. Smith said we did share in it but not to the extent of our rivals. If the details were examined, he said, it would be found that our increased trade had been largely with our Colonies, with those who had given us a preference. What increased trade did we receive? continued Councillor Roberts. During the five years 1906—i0 our exports to Germany had increased by 42 per cent., to France by 38.6 per cent., to the United States by 294 per cent., and to six other principal Protected countries by 15.2 per cent. The total average increase in our exports to Protected countries was 29/. to neutral markets (that was where we came in as Free Traders) 45/ per cent. to British East Indies 27/ per cent, and to tbe Colonies who had given us a preference in regard to tariffs 14.2 per cent.

SO MUCH FOR MR. SMITH'S ACCURACY

on that particular question. The next statement be (Councillor Roberts) extracted from the speech was with regard to the exportation of cotton goods. Mr. Smith said we imported from Germany 2 times as much as we exported to that country. The facts were that for the last year of which he bad a record-109—we exported £5,800.(100 worth Ito Germany, and imported from Germany £5,C70,000 worth. So that instead of importing 21 times as much as we exported, %a actually imported £130,000 less. That was

TARRADIDDLE NO. 2.

(Laughter). Mr Slut Tit referred to the woollen trade which, he said. was prosperous but there was no trade which stood to lose so much by maintaining our present fiscal policy. However, Mr Taylor, the member tor Radcliffe division, the bead of one of the biggest woollen indus- tries in Yorkshire, pointed out that during the five year period between 1897 and 1901 we imported into this country five million yards of cloth each year on the average, and exported an average of 48 million yards a year. In the five-year period from 1907 to 1911 we imported less than 2: million yards a year, and exported nearly 861 million yards a year. In other words we exported 31 times as much as we imported in the last of the periods, whereas in the first period we exported only GI times as much. (Applause.) That was an industry which was threatened—one of the industries which Mr. Chamberlain said a few years ago was " going." It was " going," and going splendidly too, and he hoped all our industries would continne in like proportion. (Applause).

Cockhedge Mission Band of Hope The members of the above Band of Hope who number about 370, had an unusual treat at their weekly meeting on Tuesday last. They were enter- tained by a number of children from the Wycliffe Rechabite Tent, conducted by Mrs. Hewitt. A very pretty picture was presented by them in a national tableau in which they each recited poems of the character represented and also gave national dances accompanied by Miss E, Joynson. Afterwards recita- tions and songs of temperance character were given. A short address by the president Mr. Garner. brought to an end a very happy gathering. Three cheers and hearty applause were given to Mrs. Hewitt and the children for their kindness in giving mai a treat.

JEALOUS HUSBAND. THREATENED TO KILL HIS WIFE.

At the Warrington Police Court on Tuesday, Andrew Barrow, 14, Allen-street, was summoned by his wife for persistent cruelty. It will be remembered that when the case was before the magistrates a fortnight ago the defendant created a sensation by throwing himself over the witness box. Mr Steel, who appeared for the wife, said last week's incident was an exhibition of temper which is itself would be sufficient to justify the magistrates in making a separation order against the defendant The parties had been married seven years and they had r o children. The cause of all the trouble was the ,lolent temper of the defendant.—On February 4th Mr. and Mrs. Barrow went out for a walk and the wife had said she could never be the same with him again after his cruelty. Inside his brother's house be seized two razors and said he would cut his throat, and kill her. Barrow's brother took the razors from him. On February tith they took a house in Atherton-street, and the next day the defendant violently ill-used his wife in bed. He threatened to follow her about with a revolver if she left him, and said that he would cut her fingers oft so that she could not earn her own living. Mrs. Barrow could earn lls, per week. The defendant's wages averaged from 255. to 30s. per week. The wife askecl•for an order for Bs. per week. Cross-examined by defendant Mrs. Barrow admitted that when her husband earned 30s. she received 295. He had only struck her on one occasion. She denied that her mother " had a lot to do with this." Defendant : You say I got two razors to kill you with ?—Yes. Would not one be quite sufficient ?—Yes, but you had two. Haven't I always thought a lot about you ?—I don't know. _ . .

COULD NOT EXPLAIN frIS CONDUCT.—Thomas King, of no fixed address, was charged with loitering with intent to commit a felony.—l' C Cameron stated that at 7 p.m. the previous day he saw the prisoner acting in a very suspicious manner at the rear of Gorsey-lane. He questioned him as to his behaviour and prisoner made 00 satisfactoriy reply. The case was remanded until Tuesday.

Defendant denied that he had been guilty of cruelty. He alleged that his wife had formed the friendship of several girls at Manchester, and the reason of the trouble was that she wished to go flirting about with them. Mr. Firth. Police Court Missionary, in his evidence said the real cause of the trouble was jealousy. The defendant was very much devoted to his wife. The Chairman : Is there no chance of these young people coming together again ? Mr. Steel : lam afraid not.

The Chairman said if it had not been for the defen- dant's extraordinary cooduct a fortnight ago the Bench would have been very reluctant to grant a separation, but in view of that exhibition of temper it showed that Barrow had pradtically no control over himself, and might do his wife serious injury. A separation would therefore be granted, but they would only allow the wife Is per week.

ARCHITECTURAL COMPETITION. ANOTHER QUESTION OF LOCIIL PREFERENCE.

DISCUSSION BY THE BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

_

The question of the restriction of competition for architectural designs gave rise to an interesting dis- cussion at the meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians on Wednesday. The Building Committee had considered the question of providing accommoda- tion for the male and female imbecile and epileptic patients and recommended that a building or buildings be erected on the northerly side of the Workhouse premises, adjoining the Cheshire Lines Railway, to provide accommodation for 100 male and 100 female imbecile and epileptic patients and that the committee be authorized to arrange for competitive plans being obtained from various architects and to select from the competing persons or firms one to whom the architec- tural work may be entrusted. . .

Mr. W. C. BROOKS moved and Mr. JOLLEY seconded the adoption of the minutes. Mr. FURNESS moved an amendment to the effect that plans should be invited from local architects only. Mr. liNowtEs : You make a mistake in a lot of these things. You don't get your work done as well if you limit it. Mr. FURNESS said that if they threw the competition open to the world they might have 200 or 300 designs sent in. He did not think such an enormous number was required for such a simple structure as they pro- posed to erect. They had sufficient talent in Warrington or in the Union area to supply a design and estimate suitable for the work in hand.

The Rev. A. M. MITCHELL said a question very much on all fours with that had been the subject of debate by the Education Committee with which Mr. Jolley had so much to do. He (the speaker) thought all those competitions ought to be thrown open to the public generally. He did not see why they should restrict themselves to local talent. It might be very brilliant but there might be quite as good, or even better,outside. Mr. GILL seconded Mr Furness's amendment. Mr. lisowLEs : I don't know what the building is going to cost. Will the Chairman tell us the probable expenditure ? Mr. JOLLEY : If he is a wise man he won't. (Laughter). Mr. KNOWLES said that some time ago the Assess- ment Committee wanted a valuer. They did not confine themselves to Warrington. He knew of a good many things of a similar nature in other towns and he saw what he should term pett3-fogging ideas—ideas that nothing should be got outside their own little district because a few people who would be selected for the position paid rates. If they restricted it to their own town they confined it probably to three or four people and they did not get the experience that otherwise would be obtained. If they thought national competi- tion was too wide, let them confine it to Lancashire. Mr. IlouGHTox said Mr. Furness's amendment would narrow the thing down. There were specialists in different branches of architecture end no doubt Borne had more knowledge of Local Government Board requirements than others. Mr. KNOWLES : Some Manchester men must have a wonderful knowledge of subjects of this kind. Mr. FURNESS said he thought it was common know- ledge to the Board that for the last generation they had never gone beyond one firm of architects and it was the suggestion of the Finance Committee that they should begin to broaden competition. It was thought that by giving local architects the opportunity of tendering they would be taking the initial stage and making a respectable start. With regard to Mr. Knowles' suggestion that competition should be limited to Lancashire, the principle was neither here nor there. If competition was to be extended at all on broad lines why not take in the whole of England? One was quite as logical as the other. He believed they would have from Warrington firms alcne eight or ten designs. The Local Government Board would not allow them to go in for architectural effscts that would cost a great deal of money, and local men would be quite capable of giving them all they required. On a vote being taken, the amendment in favour of local competition was carried by 10 votes to 4. Mr ABROOKS said that if the Board were not going to throw competition entirely open, why need they depart from the course they had adopted so long and with such good results ? He did not think there was any public body which had been as well-advised as that Board had been—at all events during the time he bad taken any interest in its affairs. The Board would be very much to blame as businessmen, if they adopted the policy recommended by the Building Committee and would, in the long run, be the sufferers. He moved that the old order of things be adhered to and that competition be not advised. Mr. HOUGHTON seconded.

Mr FURNESS said that in making the depar- ture the committee were not reflecting on the firm who had previously done the work. They all had the highest respect for them. Mr. Kt:owl:Es : Then what on earth do you want to change for ? Mr. FURNESS said the flrm had their confidence in every respect, but other men were inquiring why they could not have " look in" and be allowed the chance of getting some of the work which the Guardians had to give out. If the Board already had the best man, he would no doubt be successful in the competition. Mr. KNOWLES Your best man, I should think, will treat the matter as I should treat it and not compete. Mr. FURNESS : If he is in that position that he will only take work when he cannot have a monopoly, it is a condition of things which no public body should tolerate for a moment.

Mr. KNowLEs said he thought in the first place that there had probably been some hitch or dissatisfaction but they were told that their present architect gave every satisfaction. It was just as logical to ask : Why keep one man as clerk to the Board? Why not have a change? (Laughter.) He gave satisfaction but did it seem fair that he should be drawing the salary year after year when plenty of other people could do the work. (Laughter.) The same remark applied to the architect and it would apply to anyone in business. The amendment was defeated by eight votes to seven. The result is that there will be local competion for the work.

Golborne-street Baptist Church

Anniversary services were held at this church on Sunday and were largely attended. In the morning the Rev. H. Ogden was the preacher, and took for his text "Be thou faithful unto death and I will give thee a crown of life." The choir rendered the anthem, "Worship the Lord." In the afternoon there was a musical service, the Rev. H. Ogden, presiding. The following items were given ;—Miss May Isherwood, " 0. Divine Redeemer " and "Come unto him " ; Mr. W. Tynan, " My hope is in the everlasting" ; Mr. E. Walker," The Lord is my light ; duet, Mr. W. Tynan and Mr. E Walker, " Watchman ! what of the night." The Rev. W. Cooper gave the address upon " Music." Ile said that, if we were to make anything out of life at all it must be by persevering application. Music was not only helpful to worship hut in other places outside the church. We could not sing acceptably until the Lord had tuned our hearts to the melody of the skies. At the evenicg service the choir rendered two anthems. The Rev. 11. Ogden took his text from Psalm cxxii., 2,3, 4. Ile pointed out how the Jew of old went to Jerusalem, even though it meant 1,000 miles journey and the peril of death on the way. In these days it took all the power in the world to get a man across the street. If the Church had lost its grip upon man,might it not be that God had lost His grip on the Church ? At the Holy City assembled people from a hundred villages around, in one note, one song, one psalm, one purpose. Jerusalem was a compact city and what the Church of this day ought to be. Pilgrims came from East and West. From village life scattered and small they came to the one centre of unity. In business the Jew had to deal with the Baalite. To the idolater he could talk shop but not religion. He could sell or buy but not breathe his highest aspirations.Whenin Jerusalem he met souls of a similar mould. That was true to-day. Commerce was commerce, religion was religion. The mystic in business would be told that "Never the twain shall meet." That day was the anniversary of the church—a church that stood for the Sermon on the Mount in the oflice and in the w.ak. What a relief for a man with spirital aspirations to turn from stocks and shares, from problems of bread and butter, and to be recalled to the knowledge that God must be and some day would be in all the affairs of men. The church believed in brotherhood. She believed in the sanctity of human life. She believed in opportunity for the people. That was the fulfilment of the Apostles' Creed. Had a brother just gone over the brink between employment and unemployment ? The church should know and with sympathy and help remind the brother that there were hands around him. When that was the universal practice in church life men would not scorn but love her. They will say " I was glad when they said unto me' let us go into the House of the Lord.' "

New Vestry for the Parish Church.

A special meeting (says the " Parish Church Magazine ") has been held at which the chief business was the consideration of the steps to be taken for the erection of the new vestry. Four builders were nominated—Messrs Collin, Davenport, Fairclough and Warburton—and a sub-committee of five was appointed consisting of the Rector, the two wardens, Messrs. Craik and Isherwood, and the wardens' two sidesmen, Messrs. Locker and Taylor. to act on behalf of the Council. This sub-committee met the architect, Mr. Sept Owen, and his brother at the church, when it was arranged that Messrs. Owen should at once prepare specifications and invite tenders which will be laid before the Council. It is hoped that the work will be commenced after Easter.

===== 0002601_19120302_art0068.txt | included=true | included =====
Wycliffe P.S.A. society. Address on International Peace.

Mr. Cecil H. S. Wilson, headmaster of Eymm Grammar School, delivered an address on " Interni.- Canal Peace" at a meeting of the Wycliffe P.S.A. on Sunday. He said the Brotherhood movement was fast becoming an important factor in internation.l peace. During the 19 centuries of the Christian era progress had been slow, but something definite had been achieved in the abolition of warfare as a result of private feud, the practical cessation of duelling, and in later days the immensely increased use of iiAernational arbitra- tion. Still the menace of immenEe and ever increasing armaments darkened the horizon. England and Germany, to adapt Ruskin's words, purchased millions of pounds worth of panic of each other annually. The well-worn adage " If you wish for peace, prepare for war " expressed the reasonable belief and practice of 1 nnchristianised Rome, surrounded on all sides by hostile peoples, but was it worthy of Christian nations to-day ? Arbitration was cheaper, more effective, more lasting, and infinitely fairer than warfare ever could be. Among grounds for hope might he cited the spread of travel and education, and the welding together of the labour unions of the nations, but any onion of the nations which was confined to one section of the community was almost inevitably bound to end in failure, or worse than failure. They needed their journalists also to realize far more the divine nature and responsibility of their calling. Mr. Wilson then dealt with some of the difficulties which the advocates of peace have to solve and face, especially the contention that the cessation of warfare would damage the moral fibre of the nation to which it might well be replied that in the domains of poverty, crime, disease and the forces of nature there would not be lacking for the present fields enough in which man might and did display heroic qualities.

===== 0002601_19120302_art0069.txt | included=true | included =====
Photo, Burrell. THE SOUTH LANCASHIRE MEMORIAL IN THE PARISH CHURCH,

The handsome memorial tablet which has been placed in the chancel of the Parish Church to commemorate the memorable ceremony of October 6th, 1911, when the old colours of the 2nd Battalion of the South Lancashire Regiment were handed over to the care of the Rector and wardens, is the generous gift of Colonel Prendergast, who commands the battalion well-known to all soldiers as the old 82nd Regiment. The Colonel, In a letter to the Rector, after thanking him and the wardens for the impressive service with which the old colours were deposited in their last resting-place in the church, says :—" The conditions of modern war will no longer allow us to carry our colours in the forefront of the battle. But the colours of a regiment are sacred emblems of unswerving loyalty to our Sovereign and

duty to our country, and when one has been associated with each emblems for the many years that I have now served in this regiment, it is with feelings of deep regret that we part with them. I felt this very much during the service. I thank you very much for all you have done for us and your kindly interest in the ceremony; and also for your sympathetic reference to the services of the regiment, and we can only hope that when we are again called upon to servo our king and country we shall remember the unselfish spirit with which those who have gone before us did their duty and follow their splendid example." Tae handsome tablet, of which we give a picture, has been supplied by Mr. Ralph Johnson.

ANGLING.

WARRINGTON WORKINGMEN'S ASSOCIATION (N.A. CENTRE).

Members please note that rule five will be carried out this season the same as last. At the general meeting held February 12th, rule 32 was deleted. As regards opening season and the rules as to when we open, we have our own rules for our own waters, but in other waters, we must be ruled by the Northern Anglers. The opinion of members was divided as to when open- ing season should commence with the Shropshire Union Canals. It you look up the waters in your travelling card you will find the date altered from May 24th to June 15th, making the Shropshire Union Waters like all others, and once and for all settling the matter.

TIIE GOWY.

Despite unfavourable weather on Sunday morning last the Gowy had her record attendance this year. Rain fell on and off until noon was turned,then onwards until train time it was ideal and some good catches were anticipated. The anglers were, however, doomed to disappoiLtment. Here and there one, two, and up to four were taken, none of them coming up to last season's weight ; the best was J Bryers, roach lsoz. lldrs. This is the first fish on the board for the year.

SHROPSIIIRII UNION CANAL.

Several members walked across from the Gowy near the Shaker Bridge to give the canal a trial, it being more of an experiment than anything else as one or two of them had never fished here before. The report says that several fish were caught—ruffle, gudgeon and roach —none being up to the standard for weighing and they returned to the Gowy after having about two hours fishing.

NORTON.

Norton length was tri-ed-O-n-unday last. Here again the anglers had very poor sport. One or two small roach were taken and returned to the water again. This water has never found much favour for angling until after opening season comes round, but surely roach can be taken here as well as in other canals, and being an easy water for novices it is worth a second visit. ACTON BRIDGE.

Several members are inquiring if they cannot have a match for eels in this water during close season, at the same time it would give the canal a fair trial. Members could then pass their opinion as to whether the roach had made an Improvement. There is already a 105. special for eels from here and as far as the committee are concerned they would be in favour of such a contest taking place, providing the members will bring the matter forward next club night,and state what entrance fee they would like to pay. A.S.

CHURCH LADS' BRIGADE.

N.C.O.'s Cross Country Run.

A number of sergeants and corporals of the War- rington Battalion of the Church Lads' Brigade engaged in a cross country run on Saturday afternoon. A very difficult course in the Latchford district was selected. Sergeant-Major Ward (South Lancashire Regiment) started and timed the runners. After the race all were entertained to tea. In addition to Colonel Appleton and Sergeant-Major Ward, the following officers were present : —Major J A Brookes, Captains L S flenshall, Ford and I Sankey (adjutant); Lieutenants L Henshall, Johnson and Young, and the C L.B. Sergeant-Major J H Bottoraley. A concert followed, which was interspersed with speechmaking and the presentation of the prizes by the Colonel to Corporal Spencer (Stockton Heath Company), 1; Sergeant Btadshaw (Latchford Christ Church Com- pany), 2; Sergeant Goodwin (St. Paul's Company), 3; and Sergeant Williams .(Stockton Heath), " wooden spoonist." Sergeant-Major Ward said he considered it a great honour to associate himself with the brigade. He was always open to render help. (Hear, ,hear.) They were a fine lot of lads. Speaking about the after- noon's race, he said the time of the winner was very creditable, as he covered the course of about three miles in five seconds under IS minutes.

Colonel Appleton said be wished the N.C.O.'s to realize their position. In the Army, the N.C.O.'s were often termed "the backbone of the service," so those present were the backbone of the C.L.P. The sergeant-major was quite right in saying they were a fine lot of lads.

A capital programme was submitted by Lieutenant Johns-m (humorous selection), Sergeant-Major Bottomley (humorous selection), Sergeants Goodwin, Haslam and Bennett ',songs), and Corporal Gibson (two humorous songs). Sergeant Sanson presided at the piano.

HOCKEY,

LATCHFORD.

The First Eleven last week improved upon the previous season's defeat at Bowdon College by a 5-1 victory in a hard game. Howdon got a lead early on, but Travis equalised, and the score was 1-1 at half- time. Playing down the slope in the second half Latchford developed a virile attack, Travis scoring three times and Fletcher once. The visiting halves were good with Knight the most prominent. The College have a good aide, knit well together through ample practice, and the visitors did really well to win so decisively. The Second Eleven bad a very close game with Oxton "A" ending in a 1-1 draw. The defence prevailed on both sides, and in fact the forwards on each team were singularly inept in the. circles. Minards scored for Latchford before the interval and this lead was retained for some time, the home defence having eventually to capitulate to a clever shot with reversed stick by Kavenscroft. The latter player, with Mocatta and Stewart, played finely for Oxton, while for Latch- ford Pickles, Gibbs, and Bradalmw defended well. The First Eleven v West Kirby at home this after- noon is :—II Wright, goal ; P Allanson, T Dixon, backs ; F W Isherwood, J B Wilkinson, A Knight, halves ; 11 A Dale, 3 E Pearson, D Travis, W Fletcher, C Hackney, forwards. Bully oil 3.15 p.m. The Second Eleven at Tiroperley is :—Three full- backs, K Walsh, NV H Pickles, It C Parkinson ; T H Gibbs, A E Bradshaw, 'l' Banks, halves ; H R Colton, H Parker, E Banks, W Ii Minards, II Fletcher, forwards. Train from Central Station 1.35 p.m.

MINIMUM WAGES.

p

•ds Act of two, which for the first time, I believe, in this country gives the State the right to interfere in any trade or employment where it considers the prevailing conditions, judged from a national standpoint, are such as to warrant State interference. In the Act itself only four trades are mentioned, but power is given to the Board of Trade to obtain Parlia- mentary sanction to a Provisional Order ap- plying the Act to any other trade if circum- stances appear to make such a course neces- sary and expedient. That the coal mining business is one where the Act might be applied is a debatable question, but that the Government have the power to apply it is not open to doubt. The four trades named in the schedule of the Act are : Chaintnaking, machine-made lace and net making and lace curtain finishing, cardboard boxmaking, and the ready-made an wholesale bespoke tailoring trade. These four trades have been selected as the ground for the experiment, and the people engaged in them will soon have an opportunity of judging whether, on the whole, the—results are beneficial or the contrary. The central principle of the Trade Board Act is nothing more or less than this :

THE END Or FREEDOM Or CONTRACT as between employer and employe, and the establishment by law of a rate of wages to be paid for certain work, below which an em- plofer may not go without incurring liability to a heavy fine. I venture to think this is a tremendous principle, an epoch-making depar- ture from the notions which have prevailed for centuries; and I have greatly wondered why the public took so little interest in the Act while it was before Parliament and since. But, there it is, on the Statute Book, already in operation in two trades,•chainmaking, and lacemaking and finishing, and about- to be applied to two others, boxmaking and ready- made tailoring. I submit it is of vital interest to the public generally to know something of the mode of operating this new law and the effect it is producing on the trades where it is being applied. •To every employer it is important to watch how it affects wages and discipline, and consequently output; and to every worker who takes an intelligent interest in the wel- fare of his class, it is a duty to learn how far State interference in their employment has benefited them. .

I suppose it was the comparative unimport- ance of the four scheduled trades which caused such apathy amongst ordinary unin- terested citizens, but speaking for the trade I am engaged in, boxmaking, I can testify to the anxiety produced by the idea of * COMPULSORY RATES OF WAGES, and by the knowledge that, at least so far as the hire of labour 'is concerned, unrestricted control of our business has disappeared for ever. I fancy every other trade will have to , pass through the same tremors of anxiety in their turn, for it is not conceivable that such an important law, embodying such a mighty principle, will stop short with the few trades I have enumerated. If, therefore, I briefly describe how the Trades Boards Act affected boxmaking, it will perhaps serve as a model, and a forecast, of what will eventually happen in all the trades of the country. Well, to begin with, the Trade Boards Act (if I might omit the legal title I would call it the Minimum Wage Act) gave power to' the Board of Trade to establish The Paper Box Trade Board, otherwise a committee of arbitra- tion and control, and to commit to such body the duty of taking evidence and hearing coin- plaints 'from any person engaged, in the trade of paper boxmaking, and to make awards on the matters brought before them. In pur- suance of these powers the Board of Trade, for the purposes of the Act, divided England and Scotland into nine districts or constitu- ences. They then called on the employers in ' those districts to elect 16 of their number to serve as their representatives on the Trade ' Board. Similarly the workers were called on to elect 16 of their number, to act with the employers' members, and to represent the workers' interests. Four independent Mem- bers were then nominated by the Board of Trade to represent the State as it were. These ! 36 persons, who, by , the way, include both sexes, have been engaged for the past xS months deliberating upon rates of wages, hours of employment, proportion of learners to proficient workers, responsibility of the employer to thoroughly teach the trade to the learner, and all other matters likely to bear on present or future conditions of the thou- sands of people who earn their living by paper boxmaking. In these deliberations they were assisted by nine district Trade Committees, each consisting of about ten members, similarly chosen by the employers and workers in their respective districts. For it was recognised that in a widely distributed trade like boxmaking, there would not be found uniform conditions prevailing all over the country, and it was deemed only fair that every district should have a chance of making its special difficulties and requirements known to' the Board before the fate of the whole trade was settled. I. should mention that none of the elected members of the Board, of the District Committees, were paid, out-of-pocket expenses only were allowed; and having, myself, served on the Manchester Committee I can, out of my own experience, testify to the modesty, if not parsimony, of the allow- ances. I need hardly say that during the deliberations there was much ground covered , and much real hard fighting by both the workers' and the employers' nominees, and much argument was wasted by both sides trying to achieve the impossible, but eventu- ally the Board's award was arrived at with practical unanimity. That award is now published, and is to take effect on Monday, March nth, and I think a glance at its provisions will show the enormous amount •of work done, and the thoroughness with which it was performed. The award has been referred to by one eminent journalist as "The Paper Box Maker's Charter," but it might, with more propriety, be called "The Box Trade Arbitra- tion Treaty," for it not only protects the worker against had conditions and -sweated , wages, but it also

SECURES THE EMPLOYER FROM TRADE DISPUTES AND STRIKES, and relieves him from the responsibility of having to meet demands for increased wages single handed. In future, all 'such difficulties will be referred to the Trade Board, and its award will have the force of law, and, what is perhaps better, it will carry with it the united influence of the representatives of both sides. It will perhaps be of interest to give a few details of the award, so far as it deals with wages. In this connection I ought to say that it was found that no general prevalence of sweating existed in the trade. That unsatis- factory conditions did prevail in one or two districts, principally among home-workers, is undeniable, but the number of workers comprised in this class is inconsiderable, therefore, it is na,..stnall gain to the repute of the trade to have this fact established, and, also, to have it placed beyond doubt that even those few discreditable instances exist no longer. I should also mention that while the minimum rate will bring an increase of wages to many workers in the trade, it in no way- affects the wages of those who are now re- ceiving more than the minitimin. It is a levelling up, but not a levelling down. THE RATE AND PAY. The rate for ordinary workers, whether fac- tory or homeworkers, if paid by time is 3(I.

Effects of the Trade Boards Act. per hour, and if piecework rates are fixed they must be on such a scale as will enable an ordinary worker to make, on the average, at least 3d. per hour. As it is contemplated the hours Of employment will be as now—.s2 hours—this means that z3s. per week is the Minimum rate for box makers. But as rigid rule might bring hardship to elderly, or physi- cally delicate, or naturally slow workers,'es- eeptions will be permitted, but only in a small percentage of cases compared with the total number of workers. Only a certain percentage of learners will be permitted in each box factory, and the length of time spent on learning, also the wages to be paid during that period, are fixed by the awards. A certain • elasticity is per- mitted in the length of the period of learner - ship according to the age at which the learner enters the trade. Thus a girl of 14 years begins at 4s. per week, and progresses by half:yearly advances till she reaches zos. 6d.

weekly at 17% years, and on attaining the age Of IS she ceases to be a learner and becomes entitled to the minimum rate of 13S. But a girl may begin at 15 and serve only three years, or at 17 and serve only two years, or at 20 years and over,. in which case she only serves one year as a learner. The same regulations so far as practicable apply to learners already employed in the trade. I think it will be seen these conditions of labour are a great improvement, at least so far as learners are concerned, on what pre- vailed in the trade formerly, and 4 on much , which exists in other occupations now; and that they differ very widely from the old- fashionea ideas of apprenticeship is obvious. When it is added that the employer assumes the responsibility of thoroughly teaching the trade to the learner, and that she is prac- tically assured of permanent employment when she becomes proficient, it must be admitted that the present Government have, for good or ill, set up a condition of employment which will soon lead the workers in other trades -

to demand that they, too, shall have Trade Boards established for their benefit. For the effect of regulating the number .of learners will .be that everyone who learns the trade will-find employment in it when learned, no blind alley • occupations could exist if such a rule was universally applied. The Paper Box , Trade Board, therefore, may have been en- gaged in making history during the past year and a half. It has certainly done something which social reformers have long been striv- ing for, and it is .to be hoped the experiment will not be disappointing. One of the decisions gave rise to a great deal of heartburning. The award is uniform in its application to the whole of the country, no one district is treated better or worse than another; it is left to high-wage districts to rise voluntarily above the minimum, but no district, not even the most rural or remote, • can go below it. • All the foregoing remarks relate to women workers only, but the question of men's rates is due for discussion at the next meeting.. • Now, may I say a few words from TH E EMPLOYERS STANDPOINT ?

How will these Onerous c-onditini;;;•'affect hint and the capital invested in his business? One thing is clear to my mind, where the neW

rates represent an increase on the old rates they will, unless accompanied by an increased efficiency on the part of the worker, and in- creased vigilance on the part of the manage- ment, simply reduce the manufacturers' pro fit, or raise the cost of production. As it will in most cases be fatal to permit either of these two alterations to happen, he must wake a reorganisation of his business coincide with the inception of the altered rates of wages- If his business is so highly organised already that there is no margin of waste which he can save, he can only get the extra money he pays in wages by speeding up his workers. But I greatly doubt if any employer has reached such perfection of 'arrangements ia his factory that nothing remains to be saved. lii any ordinary business keen. organisation seems to be almost the last thing thought about. Most men know how to sell their pro- ductions at the highest figure, many knoW, how to buy supplies and hire labour at the lowest figures, but the number who reallY make a study of eliminating all waste of effort and of material is very few. If the State in- terference in the matter of wages compels manufacturers to organise their business more perfectly, and to pax in wages what is noW wasted in antiquated processes and in unsci- entific use of material and other ways, it may eventually prove a boon to them and to the nation at large. I feel confident it will spur the employer to make his workers more efficient, and it will compel, him to cultivate efficiency in himself and his clerical staff. It will t;ting the whole concern into greater activity to the great advantage of the trade carried on, and of the vast majority of those, engaged in it. But there is no disguising the fact that not everyone will be so. influenced- . In the trades controlled by the Trade BoardS there are, no doubt, some laggards and. some Jereiniahs, and to these minimum wages and State interference portends the "end of all things." But they are only a small minority, and their collapse is due to arrive sooner or later„; a year or two sooner will make no dif- ference to their particular trade, it will be a misfortune to themselves only. But to the enterprising and humane. employer it will he a matter of pride and encouragement to know it cannot be said any longer that people em- ployed in his line of business are getting less than a decent subsistence in return for the toil of their hands.

"4 thirt°eernin.stance, the old clock which ' • instead of one. During the

workings, the shrewd employer,—then quite a young man,—noticed that while the men promptly dropped tools as the clock struck 12, they were not so cheery to resume exactly at one. The Duke remonstrated, and a workman retorted in a stroke of humour.

At twelve, he said, there was ample opportunity to hear the strokes; at one there was the probability of not noticing it at all. The Duke had the mechanism of the clock altered and in future it struck thirteen,—an eccentricity it retains to the present day. This blending of shrewdness and eccen- tricity ran through his character and marked all his schemes. He lived and died a bachelor. From the conviction that one jilting in a life- time was enough, he gave no possible opening to womankind, even refusing (I deplore the necessity of recording this in such an assembly) to have a female domestic servant in his household. Yet he was dear to both sexes, and Man- chester and Worsley Cid him every homage. He died as he had lived, a haunter of the canal side, a dreamer of commercial dreams, and when he died in 1803, Lanchshire mourned him as a father and friend. THE DUKE'S GHOST.

No wonder that Worsley people believe that the old Duke still lingers by his canal side and tell us that if we choose the right evening we shall see his shadowy figure drawn merrily by six black horses along the quay. We would rather think of the Duke as resting from his anxieties and labours. But perhaps the Worsley people are right; for if men continue in the shades beyond the interests that enwrapped them here, the "old Duke" zmust surely be the guardian angel of canals. As I only undertook to speak of romance, I will not weary the assembly with strings of dates and figures. Perhaps I have not convinced you that romance hovers over the tranquil waters of Stockton Quay, but I hope that my disjointed address has gone to show that, whether romance or not, there is a great deal of human interest in the story of the Bridge- water Canal.

KNUTSFORD DIVISION LIBERALS. --- A Welcome to Mr. P. M. Oliver.

Every district was represented at the annual meeting of the Knutsferd Division Liberal Associaticn, on Saturday, at the Reform Club, Manchester. Mr. Frenk A. Howarth presided ard amongst others present were: Revs G A Payne and E E Ingham, Mears Philip M Oliver (prospective candidate), H P Greg, C Jackson Holmes, Henry Rol erts,J R Tomlinson, Gordon Roberts, J Longland, l'hillip M Oliver, W H Sheri:Him, E Lloyd Jones, G WallingtorP, A Albinson, II Cliffe (secretary). Apologies for absence were received from Lord Sheffield, Messrs A L Goodson, C J Holdswortb, A J King, Ziba Armitage, and others. Mr. A. J. King (ex-member for the division) wrote that his sympathies were of course entirely with them and Mr. Oliver. He beard excellent reports of him and the good work he was doing for all parts of the division, and he sincerely trusted that when the time came they would succeed in making him ths member for the division. Mr. J. R. Tomlinson submitted the annual report as follows :—" In presenting their report for 1911, your committee would congratulate you upon the results of yet another year of Liberal administration. The greatest fight of modern times has resulted in the longed-for diminution of the power of the House of Lords. Progressive legislation now has a fairer chance of becoming law, while yonr committee are convinced that the Insurance Act, subject to much criticism as it. has been, and possibly with many improvements yet to be found out during its actual working, will prove of untold blessing to the masses of this country, and will be a lasting memento of this Government, as the greatest social reform ever a tempted and carried into law. They believe it to be the duty of all citizens to do their best to secure the smooth working of the Act, and to give effect to its provisions. In view of the fact that Mr. Whitworth (who will live in our memories as a strenuous fighter and a most worthy leader at the last general elecciou) found himself unable again to cultivate the constitu- ency. your committee took steps to secure the presence in the division of a representative of Liberal hopes and aims. capable of leading us, if humanly possible, to v:ctory at the next election. Your Executive Com- mittee have, therefore, recommended Mr. P. H. Oliver, of Bowdon, to the General Council, with a view to his being eventually adopted as candidate, and your com- mittee feel they can most sincerely and heartily congratulate themselves and you upon having secured his services. He has proved himself an admirable sr:Raker, lucid, temperate and convincing. His knowledge of political questions is thorough, and his Liberalism and sympathy with forward movement and progress is beyond question. Your committee feel he would be an hononr to any constituency, and any constituency would honour itself by returning him. Daring the coming session the battle will rage round Home Rule, Welsh Dieestablishment and the proposed Reform Bill, with the long-delayed abolition of plural voting. The question of Women's Suffrage has at last entered the field of practical, though not party politics. With the change in leadership of the Unionist Party, it becomes more than ever necesary for 'Vs to be ready to defend Free Trade, and the excellent 13rancial results thereby accruing to the nation. The industries that were "going" refuse most obstinately to go. Spite of all prognostications of our opponents to the contrary, your committee are able to congratulate you upon a Budget surplus, upon record trade figures and upon decreased unemploy- ment returns, all signs of the prosperity of the country. "Mr.Cliffe has continued his excellent work as Secre- tary and Registration Agent, while the local committees may be reported generally as in good working order. In conclusion your committee feel we may go forward with confidence, knowing that the future is with us and for the progressive party, and knowing that we are working, not for selfish aims but fJr the welfare and the happiness of all the people and of the nations of the world at large." The report was adopted on the motion of Mr. Tomlinson, seconded by Mr. W. Rigby. The following officers were elected :—President,Lord Sheffield ; chairman, Mr F A Haworth ; vice-chairman, Mr A J King ; bon treasurer, Mr H P Greg ; hon secre- taries, Messrs J It Tomlinson and J Arnold Thompson. A resolution confirming the Executive Committee's invitation to Mr P M Oliver to become the prospective Liberal candidate was unanimously passed. Mr. H. Roberts, who secondecl,said that if Mr. Oliver bad created the same impression in other parts as he had done in Stockton Heath, he had become in a very short time one of the most popular men who had ever led Liberalism in the division. Mr. Oliver, responding to the invitation of the , association, said that Use National Insurance Com- missioners had a great work before them, and perhaps the greatest work was to get the doctors fairly and squarely working. If the Act meant the spending of a little more money they ought to spend it, because they must get the doctors enthusiastic about the measure. Looking back on the work of the Liberal party during the last six years, Mr Oliver said it was marvellous the progress that had been made.. With the exception of one little expedition in India, peace had been main- tained, India had been reconciled, and South Africa made an enthusiastic part of the British Empire, that laccording to competent critics the army was never more efficient, the strength of the navy maintained, and the veto of the House of Lords gone for ever. Besides all these things old age pensions had been granted, people insured against sickness and unemployment, an exten- sive grant made to agriculture and a charter given to the children. All this progress had been made with less taxation than there was in 1906. These were the credentials with which they would enter the fight in the Knutsford Division, and if they stood upon their merits they ought to get in. (Hear, hear.) Of course one newer knew what would happen. The walls of Jericho had fallen before to-day and the walls of Jericho might fall again, but if the walls did not fall, and they had to wait one or two times before they did they could at any rate keep tho fight going. (Applause). Mr. Thomas O'Donnell, M.P. for Kerry, addressed the meeting on Home Rule.

NEW ARRIVALS AT THE ZOO.

It is believed that a jaguar cub was born at the Zoological Gardens last week, but it has not yet been seen, and great care is taken not to disturb the mother. Two snow leopard cubs, about nine months old, have arrived at Liverpool from Kashmlr,and are expected to reach the Zoo and be on view there early this week.

A DELAYED MARRIAGE. The account of last week's interesting wedding at the Gospel Hall recalled the less agreeable circum- stances attending a marriage in connecticn with a similar place of worship in a town, ECHne 50 miles away, a few years ago. The Gospel Hall had been open but a year or so when it was airanged to celebrate there the marriage of a daughter of the apostle, or high priest, or ruling elder, or whatever may be the correct title of the leading lay minister. At the eleventh hour it was realised that that would fall short of legal requirements, inasmuch as the building had not been licensed fur such solemnisation, so that the religions rites must be preceded or followed by civil contract at the office of the registrar. That was a disagreeable necessity, and it was decided to get it over first; but when the parties presented themselves for that pur- pose, it was discovered that the bridegroom, who lived 30 miles away, had inadvertently left at home the document without which it was impossible for the registrar to proceed. With a view to the immediate transmission of the indispensable paper, an attempt was made to get into communication with friends on the spot, but this proved ineffective. Meanwhile the congregation at the Hall were naturally puzzled by the unexplained delay. I had gone for a 2 30 cere- mrny, and it was 5 o'clock before it was peiformed ! Really,the marriage itself did not take place until the following day. I have just been glancing over a little collection of hints to those about to " rerpetrate matrimony," and I there read—" On the wedding- day itself bade and bridegroom are viisest if they leave all the management of things to other people. A GEM FROM THE TALMUD. As one turns over the leaves of the Talmud, one ccmes across all sot. of unexpected stories, sad the experience just narrated somehow reminds me of this titbit culled therefrom :--An atheist, in an argument with a Rabbi, once said, " Your God is a thief, for according to your own Scriptures, he took a rib from Adam riming his sleep." The Rabbi's daughter asked leave to answer for her father. " Would you call a man a thief," she asked, "who took away your silver pitcher and replaced a golden one ?" "I R ish such a thief would come every night," is the answer ; whereupon the Rabbi's daughter replies, "So it was when God took Adam's rib and gave him Eve instead."

LENTEN OBSLRVANCE. An old friend of mine who still writes for the paper on which I received my early training tells of a young curate who recently got engaged to a very charming ycung lady, and in the first weeks of their newly- found bliss the young couple saw each other every day. The curate, however, during Lent, has imposed upon himself the self-sacrifice of not seeing or speaking to his fiancee for 40 days ! What the young lady says to this we are not told, but (deponent continues) this is the sort of Lenten observance that turns the whole thing into ridicule. I prefer the little boy who had to take his powders in jam, and when told by his mother that it was Lent, and asked what he would do without during Lent, as all good people did without something, said he would do with- out the powders and take, the jam. A higher Church dignitary than the one to whom allusion has been made told me that before the end of February he would be smoking his pipe on the Great Pyi amid That is another ecclesiastic's way of observing Lent. STATISTICS. I have a sort of hazy recollection of some legal luminary wittily defining evidence as (1) facts ; (2) lies; (3) statistics. This came to my mind as I read Mr. Leslie's letter on " Drunkenness in Warrington." The editorial comment thereupon was that " Figures may prove anything, but oft-times they prove nothing." In reference to a somewhat similar point, Lord Justice Fletcher Moulton went further than that in his recent address at the Sanitary Inspectors' Association meeting. He declared the glory of ancient cities like Babylon and Rome to be entirely duo to the absence of statistics.

THE CLERK AND THE ARTISAN. In the course of the Education Committee's discus- sion respecting the cost of local education, which (the cost if not education) is advancing by leaps and bounds Councillor Henshall took occasion to cite Sir John dorst, who at Manchester said "We are not raising up a generation of workers, but a generation of clerks, and not very good clerks at that." I remember the late Bishop of Ripon saying something very like it at a Public Morals Conference. Con- demning-the " snobbism "of the lower middle classes in consigning so many of their sons to the drudgery of an o ffice stool, he attributed the mischief to " a system which has been educating a nation of little clerks." Upon this a contemporaay urged that the creed. of the black coat should be discredited. It was °beet ved that we have in great measure outlived the sanctity of the black coat, but the spirit that ordained it is still with us. The clerk earning a p-und a week comes withit. the sphere of "gentility "; the carpenter earning as much a day can never hope to enter the circle. It is purely a question of calling, not of character or capacity, of refinement or intelligence. The writer confidently suggests that the career of a carpenter, a plumber, or even a bricklayer must be far more interesting than that of the ordinary clerk nay, that these trades require more intelligence. Any- how, it will be agreed that there is no reason why these and other trades should not be associated with refinement and education. The stigma upon the Britisli workman is largely due to the fact that modern conventions have divorced the two conditions. It might be thought that free schools would break down these artificial barriers. But the effect would seem to be to strengthen them. "The Literary Post " once summed up the position thus reasonably :—" Skilled handicraft ehould be no detriment to social aspira- tions. If a man is a boor, the fact of his being a bank clerk should not excuse it. On the other hand, if he is intellectually eligible to mix with people of refine- ' ment, the fact of his being a craftsman should not put him on a lower level." As the good Bishop aforesaid argued in his admirable speech, in the ladder of national life every honest occupation is as honourable as any ether. All depends upon the individual, be he manual labourer, skilled artisan, clerk, or what else.

It is sufficient comment upon the humiliating facts which emerge from the foregoing considerations that in this second decade in the twentieth century of the Christian era exponents of sanitary science have to stump the country begging and praying an indifferent populace to pay more heed to their physical well- being, and to that end inducing industrial centres like Warrington to arrange for a " health week." However, there are optimists in the field of meditine and sanitary refoirn. Prominent among these is the well- known Dr. C. W. Saleeby, the eugenics enthu4ast. He has been tracing the progress of medicine since the time of Hippocrates, 400 8.C., who believed in fresh air, plenty of water inside and out, very simple food, and not too much of it, in hard beds, and in healthy out-door exercise. With a jump we come from the time of Hippocrates to the era of Pasteur, in the nineteenth century, and he, as the discoverer of the germ thew y, represented the beginning of a new age. Mile Pasteur declared that it was within the power of mankind to abolish disease, Dr. Saleeby 11 ventures the opinion that the twentieth century will be known for having practically abolished disease. There is still lots of leeway tb make up, but War- ring on evidently means business and is bent on play- ing a worthy part in the cause of practical hygiene and all that appertains thereunto. HOME AND WOMAN'S MISSION.

Harkirg back to Lenten observances, I find that at the first of the special series of services in connection with the Warrington Church Society, the Bishop of Liverpool sought to enforce as a lesson of paramount importance an argument tentatively advanced by his Lordship's and the readers' most bumble servant who ' pens these jotting in a note arising out of the Women's Suffrage question awhile ago, viz., that our little yet great word "h )ine "—which with " mother " shares pride of place in the English language—epitomises the highest mission of the gentler sex, and that in no other sphere i 3 woman's influence at all likely ever to be so potent for the permanent benefit of the race. At the same time, of course, there are abundant oppor- tunities of usefulness for the comparatively small number of women of intellect who, unhappily as I think, have no domestic ties, and who consider them- selves morally bound to utilise their power for the good of the community at large. But perhaps lam getting on ti rather dangerous ground. After all one has to bear in mind that one is a more man. A MUSIC NOTE.

Lapsing into fiivolity, let me mention that I have just renewed an acquaintance of my boyhood. His name is F Sharp, and he is a profesQor of music. Nevertheless, people will go on asking" What's in a name ? " And will contend that the playing of my accomplished friend would be juat as sweet if he haFpened to be Professor A Flat !

THE CASE FOR IRELAND.

BY MR. JOHN REDMOND, M.P.

It is frequently urged by Unionist speakers that Ireland has no grievance because, under the Union, she has full power to send representatives to the Biitish Parliament, and that she, therefore, enjoys the full blessings of representative government under the British Constitution. The simple answer is, that the name of representative government is a mockery, unless representation be coupled with responsibility and power. At the present time Irish members have no responsibility either for legislation or administia- tion in their own country. These things are settled by a Chief Secretary, who may be i Sootchman or an Englishman, but by the practice of the Constitution, practically never an Irishman ; and his will is enforced by a House of Commons nearly six-sevenths of which is composed of British members. Measures 'elating to Ireland can be, and often have been, carried against the unanimous vote of Irish members, Unionist and Nationalist; and when a Unionist. Government is in power they are usually carried against the vote of four-fifths of the Irish representatives.

• Yet the whole system of government is separate and distinct from that of Great Britain. Education,police, Poor-law, land legislation, licensing laws —all these things are on a different basis in Ireland; and yet they cannot be modified in accordance with Irish public opinion, except by the goodwill of England and Wales. Now, however much English, Scottish and Welsh members may desire to do good in Ireland, they do not, and cannot, possess the necessary knowledge of Irish needs and conditions. They do not, for example, understand the Irish system of Primary Education; and they do not understand the special needs of higher education in a country where four- fifths of the people are Roman Catholics. They them- selves are accustomed to the presence of a locally controlled police, and they have to decide for Ireland matters relating to the use of a constabulary which is a standing army in disguise. They, and not the Irish members, have to regulate the Irish railway system, increasingly controlled by English railway companies, in the interests of England, not in the interests of Ireland. It is easy f r them, in sheer and simple ignorance, to act unjustly without the least intention of injustice. Again, a system of representative government which does not rest on the consent of the people represented is evidently a fraud. Ireland never accepted the Union. When it was first proposed in the Irish House of Commons the Bill was defeated, and under any Constitutional Government there should have been an appeal to the country. No such appeal took place. The Government simply occupied itself in Turchasing by a monstrous system of bribery the necessary majority before the second introduction of the measure Even the right of holding meetings to protest against the Uoion was denied the country. Meetings were dispersed with artillery. No one has ever pretended that the measure,when it passed,repre- Isented the will, or had the real assent, of the Irish people, Protestant or Catholic. A. rebellion against the Union broke out within three years. On its failure a constitutional agitation for Repeal of the Union was set on foot, and for a century that agitation has been carried en, with only this vat iation, that when con stitutional redress seemed absolutely hopeless, as in 1848 and 1867, the country prepared for rebellion. In 1818 rebellion was born out of the blackest misery. In the 'seventies, when Isaac Butt's Home Rule movement was begun, the c.iuntry was relatively prosperous, and English politicians argued, as they argue to-day, that it would be criminal to damage the prosperity of Ireland by giving her the right to manage her own affairs. The Home Rule demand was strong in 1880, when 100,000 people were flying ta America to escape from famine. It is no less strong to-day, when the people have become owners of their land and when emigration has been reduced to one-third of its previous rate. Nothing can be more delusive than the statement that Land Purchase has killed the demand for Home Rule. No county in Ireland has seen more extensive 1 Ind purchase than County Wexford, and yet no Unionist would ever think of presenting himself for election there. In prosperity and in misery alike Ireland has demanded the same remedy—control of her own affairs. . .

It is significant that since the Irish people got the extended franchise in 1885 the opponents of Home Rule have given up even the pretence of trying to persuade the Irish people to accept the Union, and Home Rulers have been allowed an unopposed return in all but a mere handful of the lrih constitutencies The Un'onists tried in 1885 to extend their area of operations outside the North-East corner of Ulster ; but the results were too damaging for them to risk repetition. In some counties they failed to poll 100 v tes.

If representative government means anything, stands for any substatme, it must mean that when the will of the people represented has been declared by a majority of fourth-fifths at every election for a perm' of 27 years on a given issue, the will of the people she nld prevail. Mr. Gladstone stated that the expression of public opinion in right. We adopt the great Liberal Prime Minister's axiom that a demand t xisted which it would be unjust not to meet. Since then the &Inn demand has been eight times renewed by the same overwhelm- ing majority. Every man, therefore, who believes in tepresentative and corts'itutional government must support HOlll9 Rule. Our claim is, first and foremost, rested upon an assertion of right. We adopt the great Liberal Minister's axiom that " Good government is no substitute for self-government." We claim self- government as our right, due to us, as to every other nation of white men within the Bi itish Empire. We should think ourselves unworthy of freedom if we did not claim it. But the claim is based, also, on considerations of expediency. The Union has not produced wealth, has not produced contentment, has not produced settled order, or any of the results which are indicative of good government. It is self-evident that Parliament has not time to deal with Ireland's necessities, the necessities of a nation with four millions of people. It is notorious that again and again the British Parlia- ment has refused to accede to the reasoned and invincible argument, and has subsequently conceded to violence and disorder the very reforms thus demanded the most damning charge that can be brought against any Government. Yet the British Parliament is an assembly of high fame in the world, well justified by is conduct of British affairs. Its failure to be of service to Ireland is the strongest prof that a country can never have good government until it is governed by its own people, and in accord- ar.ce with their special needs and requirements. It is a common-place of politics that the British Empire rests on a wise and generous application of the principle of self-government. Home Rule has succeeded in every part of the British Empire, and no reasonable argument has yet been brought forward to show why Home Rule will not succed in Ireland. That is " The case for Ireland." The ease against it reduces itself to this : that Parliament undoes a great wrong against which the Irish nation has strugg'ed, and under which the Irish nation has suffered for over a hundred years; if Parliament passes a measure which was carried by a House of Commons in 1893, and which since 1906 has been supported by great majorities even of British members in three succesQive Parliaments ; if a step is taken which every self-governing dominion in the British Empire advocates by the voice of its leading men, irrespective of party, and by the solemn resolution of its own free Parliament ; if, finally, the most potent cause of discord and distrust between Great Britain and the great English-speaking republic of America be removed—why, then, a section in the North-East of Ireland will refuse to obey the law, and impose their will by force, not only on four-fifths of Ireland but on the whole British Empire.

YOUNG LIBERALS.

Progress in Lancashire and Cheshire.

The Lancashire and Cheshire Federation of the National league of Young Libe-als this week com- pletes its fourth year, and the report for the past twelve months shows that 1911 saw a still more marked advance in its activities and achievements. During thl year nine new branches have been affiliated with the Federation. " The political events of the past year," says the report," have been of exceptional interest to Young Liberals. In our last report we said, The Veto must go.' It had gone, and immediately there is opened out a great vista of possibilities for social, electoral, and national reform. . . . "Electoral reform of a drastic, character has been promised. We rejoice that an opportunity will be afforded the members of the House for a free decision 'on the question of tbe inclusion of women in the scope of such reform."

LEAP YEAR AND ITS EXTRA DAY. Possibly many boys and girls have been puzzled about Leap Year, and have wondered why it should have its extra day. The explanation is that the true year is rather more than 365 days long, and we have occasionllly toput an extra dayin the calender to keep things even. It takes the earth 3651 days, eleven minutes and a few seconds to go round the sun, and the Almanac is arranged so that we have three years of 365 days and one year of 366 days. If you will figure it out you will find that this arrange- ment is hardly perfect, and in order to make it so, it was agreed that the first year of each century should not be a leap year. The years 1600, 1700, 1800 and 1900 were just ordinary years, but every other year which can be divided by four will be a leap year. To tell you how this arrangement came to be made would take quite a long article, but I can find space to say that it dates back to Pope Gregory XIII. who went carefully into the subject in 1577. Things had got so much wrong that he issued a decree annulling ten days. The rule was adopted in Roman Catholic countries but some Protestant ones refused it. It was not until New Year's Day 1600 that Scotland took the new reckon- ing and England only adopted it in 1751. By Act of Parliament it was decided that eleven days should be omitted after September 2nd and what would have been September 3rd became September 14th. The alteration did not please everybody, although it was done in the interest of accuracy, and the people raise the cry against the Government, " Give us back our eleven days." Russia still uses the old style, but always all the rest of the world uses the calender and numbe rs its days as we do.

THIS WEEK'S STORY. Mr. Marshall P. Wilder has been telling us about some stories which were the favourites of the late King. He says: One of the stories the late King Edward appreciated most was that which I related to him about a man who had a habit of walking in his sleep. One night he got up and wandered about the streets, until met by a policeman, who shook him, saying: "Here, here! I'll have to arrest you, I can't have this sort of thing." _

"But, my good man," expostulated the now thoroughly awakened sleep-walker, "this is not intentional.l am a somnambulist."

"I don't care what your religion is. You can't walk about the street in your pyjamas."
