CHAPTER X.

A MAN AND A WOMAN.

As he stood inside the- room Hawke could hardly restrain an exclamation. He didn't know whom he had expected to see. At first a mad thought had suggested that Stella had played him a practical joke, and then he re- membered that such a thing was impossible. She had never yet been to his chambers with anyone, let alone by herself. No--it was not Stella, but who was it? Then he had entered the room and had uttered the exclamation of amazement. Seated on a low couch in an attitude of indo- lent comfort was Isobel Frant! As their eyes met she laughed again, though a slight colour deepened her as yet natural one. "Surprised, I suppose, Mr. Hawke," she said, rising and holding out her hand, "sur- prised, and of course somewhat scandalised, like a properly brought up engaged young man ought to be He flushed at the raillery in her voice. After that first shock he had recovered his grip of himself, and remembered that she had perhaps come as a dangerous enemy, and he instantly took his cue from her. "Surprised isn't the word, Miss Frant. But far from being scandalised as you suggest, I am afraid my bringing up has been left in my own hands to such an extent that, notwith- standing my being what you call an engaged young man, I am quite charmed to ace you, though of course I hope nothing serious has brought you here." "When I beard your step outside in the hall," she said abruptlj, "I thought that you had brought someone in with you, and I won- dered what on earth I should say to explain my presence here." He looked at her curiously. "And that's why you laughed, I suppose? She nodded. "Yes, it would have been so funny if people had begun to gossip about us as the consequence of my being here—alone— wouldn't it? " "I should hardly call it a matter for amusement myself," he replied dryly; "let us be glad, however, that no one came in with me. By the way, did my man let you in, or had you a latchkey?" "I'm not so fortunate as to possess a latch- key of my own, let alone other people's," she retorted gaily. "But, seriously, your man let me in. I said I was your cousin and had comem e i n t oselei like rs, lamb, youoab n nmimportantr eb business," naem.s s ~, so he let "So I see !" he remarked' grimly. To tell the truth he was puzzled. He had barely no- ticed Isobel Frant before, and when he had, except on the occasion of their tete-a-tete in the Park, he had thought her rather a hand- some sullen sort of girl, with no particular charm about her, but now, as he looked at her, and listened to her assumed airiness of man- ner, he was inclined to think that there was something of the audacity and daring of a clever woman about this cousin of Stella's. She was handsome, too, in a style that did not appeal to him, but that might appeal to a hundred other men. She knew that he knew she had come to his chambers with a stronger motive than her light words conveyed, and he shot an anxious glance in the direction of the desk under the window. It was locked, and he knew that no one but himself had a key that would open it. fie turned to find that she was looking at him with a quizzical smile. "You are wondering why I came to see you! " she said. "Let me begin by telling you that I had no burglarious motive; it might relieve your mind to know that so far your secrets are in your own keeping." He laughed. "I'm not so sure of that! However, / most confess to some slight curiosity as to the reason of this visit, Miss Frant, because I am not vain enough to imagine that a frivo- lous one brought you here." "Your surmise is quite correct. I had a reason in braving Mrs. Grundy, and calling here. It was my only chance of seeing you alone, Mr. Hawke, and," she went on, eyeing him through mocking eyes, "you happen to be very interesting to me in a sense." He bowed. "You flatter me, Miss Frant. May I en- quire what natitre that interest takes? And why you should take all this trouble to tell me so?" "Becauae," she said slowly, weighing each word carefully, "I am watching a silent duel being fought between two strong men, and I am wondering which one will win. Some- times I think you will—at others I fancy that Paul Steinway might." In spite of the self-control he had over his nerves, Hawke gave a ,tart. "Steinway?" he said. "You mentioned that name to me 'before, Miss Frant. Do you know anyon" of that name?" "1 Paul Steinway to-clay lunching at th: 6moy with another man who looked like , a Colonial," she said calmly, "and I was in. terested in them because I knew that Paul Steinway was your enemy." He looked at her curiously. "Do you think I have any C 111163 to be frightened of this marvellous person, Paul Steinway?" he said with a laugh. "If I re- member rightly you referred to this indivi- dual before." She raised her head and looked at him with shining, eyes. ExciOlunt was deepening the

tndusana such mysterious aocuments bong sent to me every year. These needy devils come back to England, accidentally hear of a 'pal' who has struck lucky,' as they call it, and because they want more money and can't earn it. they start a systematic form of blackmail. I deal with such letters promptly when they fall into say possession,' he went on grimly. "I pass them into the hands of the police." She bit her lip. "But this may not have been such a case," she said slowly. "I have an idea that Paul Steinway was not the usual type of man who writes begging letters under a threat. He is your enemy—and a bitter one, and you know it, though yOu choose to laugh at it. I—l warn you against that man," she went on; I"he is a man who will succeed." He smiled.

"All this is very interesting, Miss Frant, but still I don't know why you should con- cern yourself on my behalf. May I inquire whether you have communicated your dan- gerous suspicions about Mr. Steinway to Stella?" She flushed suddenly. "Stella!" she laughed. "Stella lives in a world of her own; she is not affected by the things that affect me or you! She probably would think me mad—as you do—and then go back to her dreaming—dreams in which you and I have no place.' "What do you mean?" he said sharply. "What dream has Stella that her future hus- band does not have?" She looked at him with mocking eye's. "Need ask.? You are so clever in fsome things, surely you are not blinded by Stella's outward resignation to her coming marriage. She is in love, yes—but not with you." The veins stood out on Hawke's temple like blue cords, and for a moment wild, un- reasonable rage gripped him. He longed to strike the woman who had flung his only fail- ure in his face, but with an almost super- human effort he controlled himself, though his eyes betrayed what his will concealed. "You are making very daring statements, Miss Frant," he said quietly; 'they are of such a nature that the kindest thing I can suppose is that volt have quarrelled with your cousin, and wish to make mischief be- tween us." A scornful little laugh came from her trembling lips. "You know that I am speaking the truth. In your heart of hearts you know that Stella does not love you—that she is fighting against another love—for whom, you can guess. This afternoon she left me and took a hansom and drove off somewhere; she re- fused to let me go with her. Oh!" she panted, "do you think I would act like Stella if I were the woman you had chosen for your wife! If she were to read this letter of Paul Steinway's she would believe it at once—she would throw you over, while I—l would cling all the more to the man I loved, and if there were mud on him I would so join my life with his that I would lift some of his crime on to my shoulders!" She paused, and buried her face in her hands. She had torn the rags of convention from her, and now waited breathlessly to see the result of her mad recklessness. Would she win or lose?

The next moment she heard a sound that made her cold with mortification. Clifford Hawke opened the door, and in a cold, con- ventional voice said, for the benefit of the serrant outside:

"I will go and see about a hansom for you while you put on your gloves; I won't be a second."

The door closed behind him, and when she was alone Isobel glanced up with a white face.

"Very well," she whispered viciously, "as you have humbled me to-day, Clifford Hawke, so shall I humble you another day!"

She was glad of Lady Mary's easy flippant conversation. It helped her to 2onquer the momentary panic that had seized her at the sound of his voice. It was a fortnight since that meeting at L,ottie's, and though she had gone several times to see her, sne had never met him again, and she had been 'shamed of the depression that had gripped her when she had failed to see him.

"I have called to say 'good-bye' at last!" Tennant said, when they were all seated I gain. "The studio is empty of everything, and to-night Fifi and I seek a temporary ?mine at an hotel."

Stella looked at him with interested eyes. "Your quest? " she said, speaking for the first time.

He met her gaze for a moment. She wee tempting the faithless Fifi with a piece of ,iake, and on her white band blazed the in- :olent diamonds a Clifford Hawke.

"Yes," he replied, "I am bound by a pro- :nise to find the man who married my tister— the lady of the canvas for which Lofty was iifting, you know, and I have always let things :•Jrne in between me and my quest, but now am going to work hard and get at the truth."

"If you put it into a good detective's hande ;t should not take long," Lady Mary said briskly. "You promised to show us the papers and photographs that were sent you from Australia, but you never did!" . "And I am afraid I never shall now," he laughed ruefully; "they have been stolen from me. ' "Stolen! " both women exclaimed.

He nodded. "Yes, I found my drawer where I had kept them had been ransacked, and every Bingle proof taken away. It's the most extraordin- ary thing in the world, as nothing of any value was taken, and I had notes stuffed in another drawer.

"But have you no idea who did it? " Stella said.

"Not in the least. I did not know anyone but myself knew of their existence," he re- plied. "The day I discovered it " He paused suddenly. He remembered the day too well. It had been when she had come to the studio, and he had forgotten everything for one mad second before Clifford Hawke had taken her away. As their eyes met Stella flushed suddenly. Almost by a wave of tele- pathy she knew what the unfinished sentence was, and the memory of all it had meant rushed back upon her in a flood of regret and shame. -

"The only thing for you to do," Lady Mary broke in, "is to go to Scotland Yard." Tennant laughed. "I am going on there as soon as I leave here," he said, "but I must find someone to take care of Fifi. Poor little creature, I Alan. be going abroad after my stay in the country, and she will have to find a. new home.' .

"Let me have her," Stella said quickly. "I should love to have her—l have no dog of my own." "I should be delighted," he answered. "Fifi wouldn't know herself in such grandeur, ;f you really will have her." "Perhaps Mr. Hawke may not like you to 'lave a dog just yet, dear," Lady Mary sug- ;csted gently. "I shall not consult him," she answered with a touch of hauteur. "I will keep Fifi Always with me if M. Tennant will trust her with me." "There LI no (.7;"1 Rh" 19h9m. I. r-ould have

iiocn naa I been nekecl: lie saat quieny; 'my only fear is that Fifi will forget me all on quickly.' She looked at him with shining eyes. "I hall not let her do that," she said simply, 'and Fifi will be faithful to her first love, I :now."

"Fin will have to be shown among the other wedding presents with one of your cards on he collar round her neck, Jaspar !" Lady klary laughed, "she will create a new 'ashion in wedding presents." Stella flushed suddenly and darted a re- )roachful glance at her godmother. "Fifi shall be a personal friend—not a pre- :cut to be labelled and put in a cabinet like he others," she said gently, "and now I am fraid I must be off," she said rising. "May take Fifi with me or will she fret at first, lo you, think?" "I am afraid she might if she sees me leav- •ng, her," •he answered. "I can send her to 7ou though, Miss Traine." "I would rather you brought her," she said slowly. "Can you come to-morrow Ifternoon? Twill be alone."

His face flua-iTeil-: I 'NViII bring Fifi O you to-morrow, Miss Traine ;" he said, fter a pause, "a bone will make her forget ne for a moment. May I see you into the •arriage?" he went on; "I am also going low."

Outside by the kerb a great red car stood vaiting, and he recognised it at once. It vas the car in which she had gone away from 'he studio on that afternoon. It was, of •ourse, a present from Clifford llawke. "You mean that I may call with Fifi?" he laid in a low voice. She nodded.

"Yes, please, I shall expect you." Then for an instant her fingers rested in cool and soft, and il6 the car moved on with a sudden snort, she vanished from his -ager gaze. lie ran back into the house to say good- 'ye to Lady Mary. She was standing with a lace handkerchief in her hand.

"Stella has just left this behind," she said; 'you might take it with you When you call !Lo-morrow with the dog." Then aim her ihrewd old eyes met his she said gently, 3aspar, Jaspar, my dear boy, I'm ifraid you are very mad—but very human." Moved by a sudden impulse he gripped her hand.

"I love her!" he said hoarsely. "I have tried to keep it to myself. I'm only a penni- less artist, but I love her, and that chap isn't fit to touch her shoelace for all his money." "I know he isn't. But he is her future husband—only a few weeks now before she becomes his wife; but you can do nothing to stop it. Promise me, Jaspar, that you will not add to the burden that girl has to bear in the future?" she said earnestly. "I'm not a brute," he replied. "I am go- ing away to-morrow, but don't ask me to deny myself one last glimpse of Paradise be- fore I go!" Then in a different voice he went on, "May I leave Fifi here, Aunt Mary, while I go to Scotland Yard? I will call for her later on."

"Come back and dine," the said impul- sively. "I have a few interesting people coming in—one or two big. city magnates," she laughed. "What a pity you are not a, money-grubber, Jaspar, they might help you to become a financier."

"Thanks," he retorted gaily, "I'm afraid I have too much conscience to make a success in finance."

Half an hors later he had reached Scot- land Yard, and was ushered into a room where a quiet, gentlemanly looking man was sitting at a writing table covered with papers and rilans. It was Wellington Scafe, a detec- tive inspector of fame. After a few prelimi- nary remarks the detective said in a languid Voice: _ _

"Well, Mr. Tennant, I understood from your letter that you wanted me to find a man who came to London about twenty years ago or more, and deserted his wife? You said you had papers and other details to give me." "I had and have details," Tennant replied. "The man's name in Australia, when he mar- ried my sister, was John Ford—and that is the name they had in London, but as to the papers, including the half-finished sketch of the man done by my sister, I had them until a few days ago, when they mysteriously dis- ippeared from a drawer in my desk where they have been for years." "Disappeared!" the detective repeated, "and you don't know by what means. Do you suspect any one of the theft." No, I know of no one who knew the story, except one or two friends who were keenly interested in my finding this man. I am, of Course, awfully upset about the loss of them, because it makes it far more difficult for us to get hold of a clue." "On the contrary," was the quiet reply, "it makes it simpler, Mr. Tennant, because now we know that until the other day there was someone who was eager to get the papers, and who was in London, alive, and apparently well. I think that the first person to 'be found is the thief," he smiled ; "then we'll turn to the older affair. Have you left the studio yet?" "Yes, practically, and the furniture has gone to be stored, but will you come and look , at the place to-morrow? I have the keys, as the studio is still mine." The detective rose. "Yes, I will do that. And in the mean- time, Mr. Tennant, don't discuse your affairs with anyone. By the way, have you any idea what your sister's husband was when he left Australia?" - -

"Not the least, but he was poor, and hard up. I know so much. He had been trying his luck on the gold fields." When he left the office he felt glad that he had put the matter into competent hands, and then he dismissed it from his mind. He wanted to think about Stella and their meet- ing the next day, and he also wanted to buy some grapes and take them to lA)tty. As he made his few purchases, including a new sit- ver collar to be engraved for "Fifi," with the date of her acceptance by Stella, he felt sud- tlenlv conscious that he was being followed. lie jumped on to a Iu in riecaaniy, tnen looked behind. Yes, he had been right; he was being shadowed by the same man he had seen in the silversmith's and Benoist's—a yellow-faced Chinaman with black oblique eyes, and a slit-like mouth. THE REANE HILL MIRACLE.

We give above a Picture of Miss Dorothy whose astonishing recovery from extreme illness has been regarded by her relatives and doctor as literally a miracle. She is twenty-two years of age, and has been bedridden for five years, and a fortnight ago lost her sight and hearing. Given up as hope-

less, her death was considered a matter of only a few hours, when suddenly she re- covered her sight and hearing, and walked and talked freely. She has been taken care of by an eminent physician, who is investi- gating her case.

Stockton Heath.

WESLEYAN CONGREGATIONAL TEA.—The annual con- gregational tea was held in the schoolroom on Thursday when a large number of members of the congrega- tion and friends sat dowo. to a most substantial tea which was exceedingly well served by the following ladies : Mesdames Deakin, Ashen, Whitfield, Wilcock- son, Bishop, Clarke, Dutton, and Miss Turner, to whom a hearty vote of thanks was proposed by Mr Gill and reconded by Mr Brereton. At 7.30 a grand concert was given, arranged by Mr F E Penketh, Rev W A Beckett presiding. The chapel choir under the conductorship of Mr Penketh contributed a number of glees in a most creditable manner. Songs were nicely rendered by Misses E Jones, E Dutton, May Isherwood, Madam Lily Fairhurst, Messrs. A Forster, Levi Richardson and Cl Gough. A duet by Misses Isherwood and Woods was exceedingly well given. Mr H Webb contributed a solo on the euphonium with pleasing effect, whilst the recitals of Miss E Mosten of Lymm were greatly appreciated. Considerable humour was introduced by a laughing chorus given by the NI ale Voice Choir and a Yorkshire reading by Mr. Charles Wilcookson. The programme was exceptionally interesting and had it not been that the many items prevented any encores being allowed, judging by the applause given most of the artistes would have been recalled. Mr J Foster proved a most efficient accompanist.

Lymm.

FREE Cl-11TRCIT SERVIC E.—The united monthly ser- vice under the auspices of the Free Church Council was held in the Independent Methodist Chapel, Cherry-lane, on Thursday evening,when Mr. W. Mort gave an address on " Christ, the Life Bringer." POULTRY-BREEDING.—AS reported in our last issue Mr. H. Paget Easten, lecturer to the Cheshire County Council's College of Agriculture and Horticulture, Holmes Chapel,gave a discourse on the 22nd inst. on the various systems of houses used in poultry keeping. He also spoke on the different foods used. On Thursday he continued his course of lectures by dealing with the subject of the commonest diseases among poultry, the symptoms, causes and treatment. He concluded his interesting and instructive series by an address on " Insect pests, preventatives and cures." His lucid explanations to the questions raised were most satisfactory. _ _

COMPLIMENTARY CO NCERT. Ex-Acting-Sergeant Thomas White, at present living at the local telephone call .office, is a well known Lymm resident and since his affliction of ten years ago,when he became paralysed much public sympathy has been expressed on his behalf. Recently his eyesight commenced to fail and now we regret to say that he is nearly blind. Being a popular man this new calamity has touched the public com- passion all the more, with the practical result that every seat at the concert held on his behalf in the Congregational schoolroom on Wednes- day night last, was occupied. Mr J H Holt competently filled the office of chairman. Mr Evans, a blind gentleman of Chester,;opened the programme with a pianoforte solo. Mr. J. Lewis, another blind artist of Wrexham, sang "The tourna- ment." Mr. R. Hillier gave the coon song "Mamie May" in quite his usual style and Mr. Garrett, from Runcorn, another gentleman who bad also lost his eyesight, very satisfactorily rendered "The little hero." The humorist for the evening was Mr. Smith, of Man- chester, whose patter and songs were enthusiastically applauded. Messrs. Hughes and Lewis, two blind gen tlemen, pleased the audience with their pianoforte duet. Mr. Scott Joynt's flee bass voice was heard to advantage both in his piece "Out en the deep" and the encore. Miss Ball of Warrington, sang' May morning" in her pleasantest manner, earning loud applause and encore. After a short interval Messrs. Hughes and Evans played a pianoforte duet, the skilful performance •of which secured a well deserved encore.—As several of the artistes had to catch the train the chairman asked Mr. Gotobed to propose a vote of thanks. In doing so Mr. Gotobed said the originator of the concert was Mr. Evans, the blind pianist to whom all honour was due. Mr. Rogerson seconded the proposition which was received with hearty applause. Mr. Garrall sang " The King's Own "after which Miss Ball contributed "Sunshine and Butterflies." , The rendering of the well-known duet " Excelsior " by Messrs. Garratt and Lewis quite captivated the audience. Mr. Smith then gave a further supply of mirth. Mr. Scott Joynt contributed" My old Shacko," after which Mr. Lewis sang "My Dreams." Mr. IL Hillier's song proved to be one that suited the popular taste, for some of the young seen at the back of the room kindly provided an obligato to the chorus. The proceedings terminated with the National Anthem. The duties of accompanists were capably filled by Miss Brundrett and Messrs. Evans and 11. Saville. Mr. White is desirous of expressing his heartfelt thanks to all who so kindly came forward to his assistance. LIBERALISM IN THE ALTRINCHAM DIVISON. At the annual meeting of the Altrincham Liberal Association on Saturday, Mr W E Thompson stated that for some time a committee had been at work for the purpose of selecting a candidate to be submitted for the approval of the association. Arrangements were made, he said, to place a candidate in the field in the event of Mr Kebty-Fletcher resigning owing to the state of his health—as appeared at one time likely—but the Liberal Association hoped that the member was now so far recovered that he would retain the seat until the General Election. Mr John Alfred Morris, was elected president, Mr W E Thompson was re-elected chairman of the Executive Committee, and Mr F Milne was appointed hon. aec.

HEALTH OF THE DISTRICT. The Medical Officer of Health, in his annual report, gave the following interesting information :—The births registered in 1911 were 11l (61 males and 47 females, the birth-rate being 222. The average for the five years 1900-11 was 118, with a rate of 238. The deaths during 1911 were 61, 10 of which were registered outside the district. The death-rate per 1,000 was 122, the average for the five years 1906-11 being 13% Of the 61 deaths, 31 (over 50 per cent), were of persons 65 years of age and over. In 1911 there were four deaths of infants under 12 months, giving an infantile death- rate of 36 per 1,000. The average rate for the past five y!ars was 89 per 1,000 the average for other Urban districts of Cheshire being 102 per 1,000.

CROSS COUNTRY RUNNING

THE SOLDIERS' MEMORIAL. BATTLE OF PIETERS HILL COMMEMORATED. Memories of the anxious days of the South African War were revived on Tuesday, the twelfth anniversary of the battle of Pieters Hill, in which the men of the South Lanca- shire Regiment added lustre to the great record of the old "fighting fortieth." It was that decisive victory which opened the way for the relief of Ladysmith, and put new hopes into the hearts of millions who were daily thinking of the fate of the beleaguered garrison. The South Lancashires, amid an incessant hail of bullets, dashed up the hill with fixed bayonets and carried the position at sunset. But the victory had its melancholy as well as glorious aspect. The enemy's fire wrought great havoc in the ranks, and one of the first to fall was the beloved Colonel McCarthy O'Leary, an intrepid leader and the idol of his men. Every year, as the anniversary of Pieters Hill comes round, reverent tribute is paid to the memory of the Colonel and the many men from Lancashire who gave their lives in the campaign. On Tuesday the soldiers memorial in Queen's Gardens was once more decorated with beautiful wreaths, and during the day many military men and civilians visited the spot to pay silent homage to the gallant dead. Wreaths came from the following :—" In memoriam from the Sergeant-Major,Staff Sergeant and Sergeants of the 4th Battalion P.W.V. South Lancashire Regiment, Warrington " ; "From the officers, non-commissioned officers and men of the depot, South Lancashire Regiment " ; " South Africa 1900-1901. In memoriam, Ist Volunteer Co. Ist South Lancashire Regiment, Captain F. M. Appleton " ; "From the officers 2nd Battalion South Lancashire Regiment " ; "In memoriam, from Lieutenant-Colonel J. D. Fairclough and officers 4th Battalion South Lancashire Regiment " ; " In memory of Lieutenant-Colonel William McCarthy O'Leary and the brave men who fell with him on the 27th February, 1900. Sent by Mrs. McCarthy O'Leary " ; "From the officers, warrant officers, non-commissioned officers and men of the 2nd Battalion P.W.V. South Lancashire Regiment."

MISS KITTY PEEL AS "SOMBRA."

7.30 p.m. Messrs. Arthur Hart and Leslie Moreton are sending avery strong company, who are all of the best and the brightest. It includes that well-known comedian Mr. E. Statham Staples, late of the Whituey Theatre, London, and who has been specially engaged to play the dual role of " Mr. Smith " and " Simplicitas," aid whose singing and dancing are sure to be greatly 'admired. Mr. Staples will introduce the new song to the Arcadians, "All Down Piccadilly," which has a novel dance with full chorus. Mr. Gilbert Child (who has just returned from New York, having played the part of "Peter Doody " for over 200 nights in the States with Mr. Charles Frohman) will be found delightfully funny as the melancholy jockey. The cast will also include Miss Kitty Peel as " Sombra." The Arcadian Queen of Song, Miss Zoe Gilfillan as " Mrs. Smith," Miss Dorothy Clancy as " Chrysea " and Miss Ettie Critchley is sure to be greatly admired as :" Eileen Cavanagh," the lively lush girl, with a bit of a brogue. Mr. Harry Clark will figure as "Jack Meadows " and Mr. Cyril Delevantie as " Bobby." There will be new scenery, new songs and new dresses, making" The Arcadians" the most charming and up-to-date musical comedy visiting the provinces. A full chorus and orchestra will be under the direction of Mr. W. T. Damont. The play is produced for Messrs. Hart and Moreton by Mr. Robert C. Harcourt. Seats can now be booked at the Hippodrome or by phone No. 500.

THE WOMAN WHO WAS.

There comes a time to woman when she realises that the age approaches when she will no longer be capable of motherhood. For the moment her suffer- ings are great. But soon she will be no longer a woman in the full sense. She will be "the woman who was." During all her life she has had to suffer. It is sad to think that all this suffering has been, if she only knew, avoidable. For there is a simple, painless medicine which will give her peace and healing now in this extremity, but which would, if she had only known of it, have spared her all the sufferings of her past, from girlhood, through wifehood and mother- hood to middle age. It is Mrs. Lydia E. Pinkham's Vegetable Compound, which at every age so soothes and relaxes the female organs that it is a boon and a blessing to every woman who has used it. 'lbis can be obtained from any chemist, or post free from The Lady Manager, c/o Lydia E. Pinkham Medicine Co., 42a, Hampstead-road, London, N.W.,for 2s. 9d., or large size 4s. 6d. Mrs. Pinkham's world renowned book of private advice will be sent free of all charge from the same address, but strictly to women only.

ST. HELENS.

THE LATE MR. WILLIAM TYRER.

The funeral teok place on Monday afternoon at Anfield Crematorium of Mr. William Tyrer, of Rock House, itainhill, solicitor and registrar of St. Helens, Prescot, and the Widnes county courts. Previous to the cremation, a service was held in Prescot Parish Church. . _ .

Mr. Tvrer had been staying at Llandudno in the hope of benefiting in health, having suffered for many years from bronchitis, but owing to the cold winds which prevailed, he contracted a chill, and pneumonia developed, which resulted in his death last Thursday. to the deep sorrow of all who knew him. He leaves a widow, with whom widespread sympathy has been expressed. In the public offices held by the deceased he won the esteem of all with whom he came in con- tact, and was looked upon as a man of high dignity and charming personality. In his younger days he was a keen cricketer and a good shot, and outdoor sports of all kinds found in him a hearty supporter. Among those present at the Crematorium on the arrival of the remains were the Rev G Swainson, Messrs D Laing Beckett, J Halliwell, E Hickson, %V Case and J J Dukes.

LECTURE BY FATHER HAYDEN. CANTERBURY, PAST AND PRESENT. In an historical lecture on "The Coming of St. Augustine," at Lowe House Schools, St. Helens, on Tuesday night, Father Hayden, S.J., bade his bearers to try to picture tbe extreme bewilderment of the first Archbishop of Canterbury listening, could he return to earth, to the " charge" ef the present claimant of that title on the occasion of his annual visitation the other day at Maidstone. His grace, whose Pallium, no doubt, Augustine would like to inspect, after hearing certain startling sentences, took the opportunity to warn the Protestant parents of his diocese, and, presumably, of his province, against the practice of sending their children to Roman Catholic schools. And why ? Just because they might pick up a belief in the real presence and the supremacy of the Pope ; might come to venerate relics, ascribe some supernatural virtue to holy water, and regard, without due horror and repugnance, several other doctrines and practices, all of which St. Augustine would, of course, instantly recognise as doctrines and practices of the Universal Church brought from Rome to England by himself and his companions, and maintained and cherished by the Church he founded for nearly a thousand years—that was to say, till Henry VIII., as Dr. James Gairmer puts it, "took the bold and unprecedented step . . repudiating Papal jurisdiction, and making himself supreme head of tbe Church in his own kingdom."

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

RUNCORN BRIDGE.

UNSATISFACTORY WORKING.

A deputation from Widnes Corporation has visited Middlesborough to inspect the new transporter bridge. The deputation consisted of Alderman F Neil (Mayor of Widnes), Alderman S Owens (chairman of the Finance Committee), the Town Clerk of Widnes, Mr Henderson, the general manager of the Widnes and Runcorn transporter bridge and Mr Basil Mott, C.N., of Westminster, London, the consulting engineer of the Widnes Corporation. It is stated that the Widnes and Runcorn transporter bridge has worked unsatisfactorily of late and Mr Mott has been called in to advise the Widnes Corporation as to the reconstruction of the working arrangements. Before finally deciding the question, however, it was agreed to send a deputation to inspect the recently constructed bridge at Middlesborough.

===== 0002601_19120302_art0118.txt | included=true | included =====
A WOMAN'S AIM.

Ellen Doyle, of Water-street, Runcorn, was 'charged on Monday, with having assaulted Mary Jackson, of Brunswick-street. Mrs. Jackson, whose face was covered with sticking-plaster, said that on Saturday afternoon the prisoner threw a saucepan at her, which hit her on the jaw, causing a wound that had to be stitched. The prisoner said the saucepan missed its mark when first thrown, and the complainant seized it and beat her about the head with it. She threw it a second time, and got home on the complainant's jaw. Sarah Lloyd, Cooper-street, said the saucepan was thrown three times. The prisoner also hurled a plate at Mrs. Jackson, which she avoided by bending down. Then the prisoner got a poker out of the house and threatened to brain the complainant with it. The bench dismissed the case.

===== 0002601_19120302_art0119.txt | included=true | included =====
RUNCORN RURAL DISTRICT COUNCIL.

The fortnightly meeting of the Runcorn Rural District Council was held on Tuesday afternoon at Dutton Workhouse, with Mr Thomas Wright in the chair.

STRETTON SEWERAGE. A meeting of this Committee was held on the 16th inst. The question of the desirability of sewering the Parish of Stratton was discussed and the Committee pointed out to the representatives of the various estates that it would not be possible in view of the small assessable value of the parish to provide a scheme at the expense of the rates, and if the owners desired a scheme it would be necessary for them to jointly contribute such a sum as would be sufficient to construct the works. Mr. Melville undertook to recommend a contribution from the Lyon Estate equal to one-half the cost of such a scheme as would embrace the whole of Higher Stretton. Messrs. Gandy, Sankey, Westbrook, and Dr. Bower also undertook to recommend contributionsfrom the estates in which they were interested. The Com- mittee resolved to take the question of It sewerage scheme into consideration and to call a further meeting at an early date. The report was adopted. APPLETON WATER SUPPLY. _

scheme

Mr Bridge i;;;e7l6-a.t the Warrington Water Main should be continued by Appleton across Pepper-street and by the village school and cease. It was decided that the Council enter negotiations for the main to be continued from Dorothy Farm, Stretton, to a point opposite Thorn Brow Farm. RUNCORN BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

The fortnightly meeting of the Runcorn Board of Guardians was held at Dutton on Tuesday afternoon, Mr. Joseph Spruce presiding. THE LATE MR. FRODSHAM. The Chairman at the commencement of the meeting, touched on the death of Mr. John Frodsham who had for some time been a collector for the Board. It was decided that a letter of condolence be sent from the Bosrd to the widow. THE CHILDREN'S HOME. The Local Government Board wrote with reference to the use of Dutton Lodge as a home for children that upon being furnished with a definite assurance from the Guardians' legal adviser that there were no exceptions, reservations, restrictions or restrictive covenants affecting the property or interfering with its free use for the purpose intended, the Board would be prepared to authorise the hiring of the premises by the Guardians. A memorandum was attached respecting minor altera- tions and the Guardians amended their previous recommendations that a brick wall be constructed as a division between the boys and girls' quarters, but adhered to the proposal that a butler's pantry be included in the plan. THE WORD "PAUPER." The Bedwelty Union (Mon) sent a copy of resolution passed at their last meeting stating that in their opinion the time had arrived when the word "pauper " should not be used when speaking of the chargeable poor of this country. Mr. Linaker (jun) said he would like to know what name they were going to substitute. There was no suggestion of an alternative. Mr. Walter Morris : What next? What with con- certs in prisons we shall soon have Utopia. Mr. Linaker (sen) said the word " pauper " was a most offensive one and in some cases was very undeserved. Mr. Baxter said tbat the word had a deterrent effect on the undeserving poor. Alderman Norman: .1 hope Mr Baxter does not sug- gest that all the poor are undeserving. The Clerk pointed out that the word was very rarely used except in resolutions. There was no official designation for poor persona. The Board decided to support the resolution. COMPULSORY DETENTION OF TIITHISICAL CASES. The Stockport Union asked the Board's consideration of a resolution passed by them that it was desirable that further powers should be conferred upon Boards of Guardians enabling them to detain persons suffering from pulmonary tuberculosis who may be addmitted to sanatoria or similar institutions provided by Boards of Guardians for the treatment of consumptives. Mr Woodward was of the opinion that the Guardians should have the power to restrict and that those suffer- ing should be under some restraint. It was decided to support the resolution.

NEW ORGANIST FOR ST. HELENS. Mr. Beckett, A.R.C.0., of Rotherham, has been appointed organist and choirmaster of St. Helena Parish Church, in succession to Mr. E. Haywood, who recently went to be organist at a cathedral in New Zealand. Mr. Beckett is well known in Oxton, where he was organist up to about two years ago.

THE SOCIAL POLICY OF THE GOVERNMENT. But the election of 1906, with its unparalleled over- throw of Toryism, made it clear that the reign of laissez faire was at an end. Mr Chamberlain had offered a return to Protection as the solution of the !social problem ; bet the proposal only aggravated the Tory defeat. The alternative solution offered by the Liberal Party was that of internal re-organisation under the general title of Social Reform. This solution involved a radical departure from the individualist tradition of the Liberal Party. It meant the definite intervention of the State in the sphere of social and industrial conditions and the final abandonment of the old doctrine that the duty of the State was to keep the ring for the competitive struggle.—A. G. Gardiner, in the "Contemporary Review " for March.

Eve yn-street School Headmastership. Sir,—Mr. Upson's letter on the above subject in your lat issue 'does not carry conviction as is usual when Mr. Upson uses his pen. Does he mean us to infer that because in business appointments means are not always taken to get the best man, this is a good reason .w hy the Education Committee, a, respon- sible public body, should follow suit? Further (if 1 disentangle his argument aright from the sarcasms in which it is enveloped) be reasons because other large towns,in which there is a much wider field of selection, restrict their selection, Warrington should copy their bad example. Mr. Upson usually manifeste such independence of naind, and clearness of judgment, that it surprises us that he should advocate following the multitude to do evil. He praises, and deservedly, the merits and qualifications of the Warrington teachers. 'then he very pertinently asks Why do the Warrington teachers fear open competition ?" This is the question in connection with the Evelyn-street appointment that everyone is asking. Mr. Epson's reply is that "there is no such thing as open competition" and Proceeds to show, not that the competition for Evelyn-street would not be open, but that this condition does not exist in certain restricted areas, an entirely different thing. Look at the question from the educational point of view. All the teachers in Warrington are free to apply for any position elsewhere in the large free area; the teachers in voluntary schools, which con- stitute a large majority of the Warrington teachers, are free to apply for positions in voluntary schools even in restricted areas. It is common for Warring- ton to lose teachers (we have just engaged one for the headship of the Penketh school) ; the managers wish these well, congratulate them on their improved posi- tions, and usually proceed to fill up by appointing some old scholar. By this process the average teach- ing capacity of the Warrington staff is lowered because it is as a rule the most capable, who because of their capacity, secure better appointments elsewhere. Unless vacancies arise when occasion,requires same to be filled from outside, the standard is reduced. We do not assert that there is not the "beat" man for Evelyn-ptreet already in the town. What we say is let it be put to the test without fear, favour, prejudice or influence. We object to restricting the area of selection f.,r one grade because it creates a precedent which may be logically extended until all grades are " protected," and the Committee have no opportunity of infusing new life into the teaching staff from outside. 1 have always been a firm believer in Hungarian partridges,Mongolian pheasants, Hereford cattle, and Clydesdale horses. It is easy to' gain a cheap and paltry popularity by shouting" Warrington for the Warringtonians " but those who are prepared to welcome the best from out- side stand for the true interests of the town and of the children. Sevetal Warrington schools have got stereotyped in their methods, controlled by tradition and routine, because the staff is only sustained by moves up. Hence we have in Warrington under the same authority, with a similar class of children, schools varying considerably in their standard of efficiency. Some schools have got becalmed in a sluggish back water. A change of staff emanating from the managers would be a nine days' wonder, and a change becomes necessary only in the rare case of a teacher making a move off his own bat, and this is seldom made to gain more experience unless it also means a bigger salary. Warrington teachers need mixing up,shaking up - and I was going to add—waking up. What I mean is with few exceptions (as far as any expression is a guide) there is no general desire for a change of schools for the purpose of gaining a wider experience and more varied knowledge of teaching. There is an unhealthy contentment, a readiness to remain in the same groove year after year. Would it not be an invigorating movement if every under-teacher were brought under the influence of a new head every two or three years ? It would be better for the teacher, and better for the schools. _ _

There is a game called "Family coach "I am obliged to take part in whenever we have a children's party. Each player has a seat and takes the name of one of the articles in connection with the coach. A player is in the centre blindfolded, whose object is to secure a vacant. seat; one calls the changes to be made, "The whip and the brake," "The reins and the wheels," &c. When " Family Coach" is called all change seats. I should like to see the Education Committee call "Family coach" amongst the teachers in Warrington.Some of the schools have great need of the infusion of new blood, even if it were only from a neighbouring school. It would be well for education if some national authority would say "Family coach." it would be still better, were it practical, if we could have a general international interchange. Like Mr. Upson I have formed a high estimate of the Warring- ton teachers, and the Warriugtoa teachers have formed a high estimate of themselves : this is a merit, also a danger. A danger which may lead to disastrous consequences, if as a result, important Warrington posts are filled by " Warrington lads" to the exclusion of better men from out.ide. We can reasonably expect that picked teachers who have trained in a different environment would introduce, not into one rchool only, but gradually through the whole Warrington school area, better methods, and fresh ideas. In a word we need to repeat again and again the steps taken which brought Mr. John Upson as an outsider into the educational activities of Warrington. CHARLES E. PARKER. Penketh, March 14th, 1912.

Exports of Cycles. Councillor Bullion Replies to Mr. C. E. Parker.

Sir,—l have read Mr. C. E. Parker's article, re ' export of cycles, with very great interest. As a theoretical way of trying to make a bad case good, it probably answers its purpose (to those who are not in the know). Perhaps Mr. Parker will pardon me if I say that he has not given a single correct reason why the cycle trade is in its present position. The principal reason is this : We have retained to ourselves the reputa- tion of making the hignest grade cycle in the world, from the time it was manufactured commercially. What is more convincing is the fact that any firm in this country who attempted to copy the foreign dumped cycle, without exception, signed their death warrant. Havinghad over 20 years' practical experience, it may be interesting if I give a brief ac-,•ount of the rise and fall and rise again of a portion of this trade. I believe if anyone likes to make careful inquiries from anyone of importance in the trade be can easily verify my statements. It will also help to drive another nail in the coffin of theoretical arguments on Free Trade. I can remember cycles being made in Warrington 40 years ago by private people for their own use, and mention Mr. Milner, Mr. Greening and my father, as perhaps the best known. From efforts of this kind all over the country was evolved the making of the first spider bicycle. This was really the birth of the cycle as a great commercial article. From this developed the Kangaroo and Facile, both front-wheel gear-driven cycles, and afterwards the present back-driven type, with, of course, hundreds of freaks between them in the evolution. One of the great factors in our retention of the trade is that every important improvement in the cycle is the invention of Britishers. The most notable are the spider wheel, rubber tyres, ball bearings, chain drive, the present safety bicycle, pneumatic tyres, &c., &c. Coming now to the periods under discussion by Mr. Parker I might say we have to thank Royalty for the extraordinary demand that was created at the time we imported the most. To parody a phrase to explain this, say "What Royalty does to-day Great Britain does to-morrow." The manufacturers were at this time in the happy position of being unable to supply the demand. Several important importers of foreign goods commenced to bring over American cycles by the ship load, and even then it took a year or two to keep pace. The imported cycle Was a freak compared to our own, being minus the necessary refinements, such as detachable tyres, brakes, proper mud-guards and gear cases; Stc.„&c. The only virtue it had was thaut,of being low' priced. During this boom the financier became extremely busy and fortunes galore were made out of company

promoting. At this time (unfortunately for investors) the aristocratic e7clist's interest commenced to wane in favour of motoring, and as the demand in this country commenced to drop the supply slowly exceeded the demand,and in the knowledge of everyone,first one factory and then another closec, down, until it became the .survival of the fittest. History proves that the manufacturers to-day are the firms who retained the excellence of their goods and who had not neglected to develop their sales abroad, at a time when most firms were (to use a slang term) suffering from swelled head. To prove the disorganized and demoralized state of the trade, I ought to say that every firm of importance had to go through a course of reconstruction. Cycles were a glut ,in our matket and it was the foreigners' opportunity to buy. They did, and finding the qnality of our goods so much sunerior to any others the demand increased. Our manufacturers immedi- ately adapted themselves to the altered conditions, with the result that our misfortunes were the foundation-stones of cur present position. There was a phase in the trade that might have a very slight bearing on the questions of Free Trade or Tariff Reform. Several firms opened works on the Continent and eventually imported into this country a lot of the most intrinsic components. These were used in the manufacture of several " English cycles," and there was a great outcry about it. I merely Mention this as a condition that did and probably does exist to-day, but it does not affect the position of the industry as a whole with regard to its export trade to-day. There are, I know, many trades that are being killed by Free Trade. I also consider it unfair to use such arguments as we find in the article on the "Export of Cycles," until sufficient proof has been found that Free Trade or Tariff Reform was the direct cause of its rise or fall. I must apologise for taking so much of your valuable space, but it is necetsary, so as to make an explanation that can be understood by everyone of your readers.—Yours &c., W. 11. BELLIAN. The Spinney, Grappenhall.

GREAT COAL STRIKE.

ONE MILLION MINERS IDLE.

The great coal war in now in progress and over one million colliers and surfacemen are on strike..

The stoppage generally took place quietly on Thursday, and all reports agree that the miners have entered upon the struggle in a peaceful spirit,with the intention of maintaining that disposition until the dispute terminates. A hopeful sign was that many of the miners left their tools underground. The strike has already begun to dislocate other trades. From all parts of the country come reports that works are closing down, and the railway com- panies are announcing curtailed services, and are placing large numbers of men under notice. The situation has taken a dramatic turn, and it is hoped that national disaster will be averted by bold and drastic measures.

Impressed by the justice of the miners' case for a minimum wage, the Government have decided to establish the principle by law, subject to certain conditions and guarantees. Failing agreement between miners and coalowners, a Bill will be introduced in the House of Commons next Monday. Mr. Asquith announced the Govenunent's decision to the Miners' Federation on Thursday in one of the most masterly brilliant speeches ever delivered by a statesman, lie made a patriotic appeal to the delegates to allow a reasonable latitude of negotiation as to the figures of the district minima, and emphasised the " terrible responsibility " of either of the parties standing in the way of a reasonable settlement. The negotiations for a settlement were resumed yesterday.

===== 0002601_19120302_art0124.txt | included=true | included =====
NEGOTIATIONS SUSPENDED.

Last evening it was stated that all negotiations between the coalowners, the miners' representatives, and the Government have been suspended until next week. The Press Association says :—lt is generally believed among miners' delegates that the strike cannot be arrested Under a fortnight.

===== 0002601_19120302_art0125.txt | included=true | included =====
THE POSITION IN WARRINGTON.

Works Closing Down. A prolonged strike in the coal trade would have most disastrous effects in Warrington, which is entirely dependent upon its industries. The stoppage of the fuel supply must speedily bring the large works to a standstill for it is impossible for them to stock efficiently large reserves to tide them over any great length of time. Some firms are already feeling the pinch, with the result that large numbers of work- people will find themselves cut of employment this week-end. Should the strike last over a week the ranks of the workless will probably reach above 5,000. At the huge works of the Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Company, Ltd., the effect of the deadlock has been swift and pronounced. To-day two-thirds of the work will be closed down, and con- sequently about 1,500 men will be thrown idle. The remaining departments will be kept open as long as possible but if there is not a speedy settlement it will inevitably become necessary to cease work altogether. At Messrs. Rylands Bros., Lid, wire manufacturers, Church-street, the inconvenience of the situation has soon made itself felt. A section of the works, involv- ing 700 or 800 employees, was closed last night, but iit s hoped that it will be possible to run several departments for a fortnight. At Whitecrosa works the rolling mill has been closed and, unless events take an unexpected turn, the probability is that the whole of works will soon have to follow suit. Messrs. T. Crosfield and Sons, Limited intend to close down very gradually, but if the trouble extends over a prolonged period they will have to economise until only those departments which are absolutely essential to the business are running. Messrs. Monks, Hall and Co. Limited do not anticipate that the strike will affect them loony great extent for some time. Ile work- people of the Firth Company will be placed on shorter time next week. "What will happen afterwards, depends upon the strike" we were informed. Messrs. W. D. Houghton and Co., Ltd., state that, unless railway traffic is seriously dislocated, they will be able to continue working for several days without dispensing with any hands. Messrs. Greening and Sons, Ltd.. the Longford Wire, Iron and Steel Co., Ltd., Messrs. Fletcher, Itussell and Co., Ltd., do not anticipate any serious interference with their work for the time being, but a great deal will depend upon the means of transport. The various Corporation undertakings are well- prepared to cope with the situation. The Chairman of the Water Committee stated at a meeting of the General Purposes Committee on Monday evening that a stock of coal sufficient to last for three months had been laid in at the Waterworks. The Electricity and Tramways Committee have accumulated enough to last for a month or five weeks and the Gas Depart- ment is similarly well-provided for. The Mayor (Councillor Dr. Joseph,) attended the hastily convened conference of Lord Mayors and Mayors held in London on Saturday, when a resolution urgently calling for a settlement Of the crisis was passed. In consequence of the coal strike, railway' companies have greatly curtailed their services.

===== 0002601_19120302_art0126.txt | included=true | included =====
'POLICE ARRANGEMENTS.

Detachments of Warrington police are holding themselves in readiness for strike duty in the Nottingham and South West Lancashire coal fields. It is expected that, as in the case of the railway strike. the conditions in Warrington will be peaceable and orderly. Yesterday, fifty Warrington employees of the London and North-Western Railway Company were swor.n iii as special constables, in the event of their services being needed for the protection of the Company's property.

Death of Mr. John Church. An old and valued servant of the Corporation passed away on Tuesday in the person of Mr. John Church, of 121, Wilderspool-causeway. He was sixty-eight years of age, and had been the foreman in tbe Sani- tary Works Department of the Corporation since November 27th, 1876. He had been in failing health for some time, and a fortnight since was taken ill in the office, and had to be removed home. He leaves al widow and several children.

AT APPLE roN HALL.

Photo. Burrell. MEET OF THE CHESHIRE HOUNDS AT APPLETON HALL ON WEDNESDAY.

are more important than the dead ones, that men stand for more than money. (Hear, hear.) Service given by labour establishes a claim, a right, to national appreciation and consideratitn. The welfare of the whole must stand before the wealth of a part ; human rights are greater than capital rights. (Applause.) These Acts are a step fore ard towards that position in which it will be acknowledged that useful service of whatever kind must be the test of the claim which any individual, or any class, may present for a share of national wealth and esteem. The cheques crossed by the word " service " should be the first to he honoured —in fact, I am coming to the conclusion that they should be the only ones that ought to be honoured. (Hear, hear.) So far we have not attained that position, but we are moving thitherward, and both the Old Age Pension Act bind the National Insurance Act are big forward steps. Even if these Acts in their operation meant that the share of wealth which usually goes to I the possessors of wealth was diminished, I should still be proud to be associated with the party that was wise enough to give expressson to the principles of these Acts.

But are we sure that the State help given, say in the Insurance Bill, must necessarily be at the expense of the earnings of capital? This is a question which the future alone can definitely settle. But lam inclined to think that there is a very great deal in the contention made by Mr. Lloyd George and other advocates for this Act, that in taking steps to guarantee greater efficiency amongst the workers we are taking the

SUREST - MEANS TO INCREASE THE EARNING POWER of our industrial undertakings, that keeping workers fit in the best way to make both work and capital effective instruments of production. (Hear. hear,) And it can be reasonably expected that the outlay made by the State and by the employer will come back to both these bringing its increase with it. But whether this be so, or no, is after all a secondary question. The giving of the pensions, the granting of State aid to sickness by the Insurance Act was called for by higher consider- ations than the mercantile expectations that it would bring back to the contributaries more than its money value. The tendency which has expressed itself in these Acts is to strengthen the weaker individuals. This legislation is not a movement towards State manage- ment, or the suspension of competition as some contend but rather towards the making of this competition fairer by strengthening the weaker units and enabling them to take their part on more equal terms. A con- siderable proportion of our people take part in the rivalry of life under conditions which preclude them from any real chance. They are the victims instead of being the heirs of our civilisation. We want every Englishman, woman, and child to

SHARE IN THE PROSPERITY, in the wellbeing of our country. We want to bring the humblest to the banquet of civilization. Now 1 hold that a fundamental impetus In all progress must be personal rivalry, and the aim of legislation ought to be, not to abolish, but to raise this rivalry to the highest degree of eMciency by bringing all into it on a footing of equality of opportunity, allowing the widest possible scope for the development of every individual fscult and personality. (Hear. hear,) That is the goal ty which all sJund legislation should tend. This is tho direction which the two great Acts of our Liberale Government take, and I am proud, and you all ought to be proud, to beassociated with a party that was strong enough and wise enough to make law, both the Old Age Pensions Act and the National Insurance Aot.(Applause)

LIBERAL ASSOCIATION.

ENTHUSIASM AT TNE ANNUAL MEETING.

THE PRESIDENT AND THE INSURANCE AT

Attu. The enthusiasm which pervades all branches of the Liberal forces in Warrington was strikingly mani- fested at the annual meeting of the General Council of the Warrington Liheial Association held at the Liberal Club on Thursday evening. The attendance was one cf the largest ever seen at a business gather- ing and there was evidence of a healthy determination to regain the seat far Liberalism at the first oppor- tunity. Mr. C. E. Parker (president of the association) presided and amongst others present were : —Alderman Tinnion, Councillors J Wilkinson (chairman of the executive committee), J T Locker (ben secrtary), J Dickenson and J Flalliwell, Messrs 'l' Pemberton (hon. treasurer), P Pesci ck, H G Roberts, E Lloyd Jones, E Wynne Jones, J Critchley, W Bibby, E V Walker, P Delaney, E J Hualies. Mr Howarth, P II Brown. T F Wallington, T Dale. W Hayes, P O'Brien, C Healey, EA Sanders and Harry New. The follow- ing representatives of the Women's Liberal Associa-. tion weie also in attends, cc :—Mrs E Osborne (hon. secretary), Miss Shout, Mrs Dutton, Mrs Delaney and Mrs Booth.

Letters of apology for non-attendance had been received from Messrs W Clega, G Weaver and J Burns.

THE WORK OF THE YEAR. Councillor LocKstu presented the report as follows:— 'fire past year has been a fairly busy one, and we can claim to have done a little towards making our organise tion a more lively and efficient one. The Interest of the members has been well sustained. During the early months of the year several interesting Ward meetings were held at the Liberal Club at which Mr. New delivered addresses on current topics. These meetings were well attended and much appreciated. During the 'summer months about 20 open air meetings were bola in all the various parts of the Own at which the attend- ances ranged from 100 to 1,000 persons. At all thOe meetings a goodly quantity of literature was distri- buted. A spacious lecture van was purchased and formally presented to the Association by Mrs Crosfleld at Bridge Foot, on Saturday, July 29th. A great mass meeting was held at which Mr. A. H. Crosileld and Mr. F. Maddison delivered stirring speeches, our President, Mr. C. E. Parker, being in the chair, supported by the leading Liberals of tire town. The attendance on thlit occasion was the largest ever seen at an open air meeting locally. (Applause). On the following Thurs- day an interesting debate took place at Bridge Foot on " Free Trade v. Tariff Reform " Mr. R. Hensball, Manchester, advocating the latter policy and Mr New championing our cause before an audience of about 4,0E0. During the summer months various wards held their bowling mains with consider- able success, and very pleasant functions re,.ulted. During the past three months the social life of our association has been well sustained, six of our ward committees have been entertained to supper at the cafes by their representatives and chairmen, the average attendance being 98. Whilst we are glad to see so many attend these pleasant socials, we should be delighted to have them with us more often. A series of interest- ing lantern lectures were given at the Liberal Club on January 8-13, Mr. New lecturing on Six years of Liberal Government,"Mr.P. Peacock generously defray- ing the cost. These lectures were, however, not as well supported as they deserved. Arrangements are now in progress for

AN ACTIVE CAMPAIGN extending over a period of three months. (Applause.) Meetings will be held in every ward to enable the National Insurance Act and Home Rule proposals to be fully explained. A mass meeting will be held at the Co-operative Hall on April Ist, and one of the leading members of Parliament will address the meeting. lam glad to note that the Junior Liberal Association has been revived and a fine body of young men brought together who, with our support, I am sure will prove a splendid asset to the cause of progress. (Applania ) Our registration and survey work must have more time devoted to it, and although Orford, Whitecross end Town Hall wards are up to the mark, special attention must be given during April, May and June. We are greatly handicapped with lodger claims, owing to the ratable value fixed in Warrington being higher than elsewhere, £2O being the value required for a lodger claim, and the Revising barrister is especially rieid on this point. At the municipal elections our candidates, Councillors Roberts and Halliwell headed the poll with increased majorities—(applause)—whilst our friend Councillor Dickinson was returned unopposed. In conclusion gentlemen, I desire to express my thanks for all the support you have so generously given me in the past, and assure you that whenever required, my services are at your command. (Applause.) Especially am I grateful to the officers and various committees for their assistance in the cause we all have so much at heart and the good work that is being accomplished leads me to look forward confidently to the future, believing that the united effort of the pro- gressives will once again carry our friend and leader, Mr A H Crosfield to victory on the next occasion. (Applause.) His splendid work for our cause is well known to us all, and on behalf of our association I appeal to every Liberal to do his utmost in winning back Warrington to Liberalism. (Applause). The report was adopted on the proposition of Mr. C. HEALEY seconded by Councillor HALLIWELL and supported by Alderman TINNION. THE FINANCES. The balance sheet, which was submitted by Mr. T. Pemberton, showed the finances of the Association to be in a healthy condition. Mr. H.G.ROBERTS moved the adoption of the balance sheet and warmly congratulated Mr. Pemberton on the encouraging results of his work. Mr. P. O'BurEN seconded and the resolution was agreed to. ELECTION OF PRESIDENT. ADDRESS ON THE INSURANCE ACT. Mr. P. DELANEY, in proposing the re-election of Mr. C. E. Parker as president, said they could not have a better man to lead them. He was always willing to assist financially and with service,and they were greatly indebted to him for his untiring advocacy of the Liberal cause. (Applause). Mr. E. I.l7vYNk JONES seconded, Alderman TINNION supported, and the resolution was passed with much enthusiasm. Mr. C. E. PARKER, after expressing his gratification , at the unity and enthusiasm of the party, referred to the National Insurance Aot which, he said, was the corollary of the Old Age Pensions Act. It is fitting, he said, that when the eye becomes dim and the natural force abates the veterans in the industrial army should have provided for them something for their comfort in old age. But we well know that there are a considerable number of workers who, because of ill-health have, for longer or shorter periods, to fall out of the ranks long before they have reached the three score and ten limit. Thie Act is an acknowledgement, not perhaps as emphatic as it ought to be, and will be, that HUMAN INTERESTS MUST TAKE THE DOMINATING POSITION IN INDUSTRIAL OPERATIONS. (Applause.) It is an acknowledgment, partial but still important, that the living factors of production

TIM HON. TREASURER.

Alderman TINNION, in proposing the re-election of Mr T. Pemberton as bon. treasurer, said he bad been associated with him in other work on behalf of the Liberal Party and in connection with another organize tion of which he was treasurer and he believed that Mr Pemberton was, without exception, the best man in Warrington for the position. (Applause.) His accounts were absolutely perfect and he took a real pleasure in the work.

Mr. N.. WYNNE JONES seconded and said they were very fortunate to have a gentlemen of Mr. Pemberton s experience to fulfil the duties. (Applause). The proposition was heartily approved.

THE HON. SECRETARY.

Mr P DELANEY proposed the re-election of Councillo Locker as hon. secretary. Words were, he said unnecessary to commend him for the position which he had so honourably Occupied in the past. (Applause). Mr J CRITCHLEY seconded and said that in Coun- cillor Locker they had a very worthy secretary. (Applause). Councillor LOCKER. in returning thanks, said be was only too proud to give his services for the advance- ment of Liberalism in Warrington and it was kis keenest desire to be in touch with every unit of the party. (Applause). CHAIRMAN OF EXECUTIVE.

Councillor WILKINSON tendered his resignation of the chairmanship of the Executive Committee,pointing out that he had filled the position for four or five years and the pressure of municipal and other duties made great demands upon his time. He proposed Mr Peter Peacock as his successor. Mr O'BRIEN, in seconding, said they greatly regretted Councillor Wilkinson's resignation but hoped he would continue his active association with the party. The PRESIDENT said the Association was under a considerable obligation to Councillor Wilkinson for having held a position of considerable responsibility, and involving a great deal of thought and bard work, for the past four years. They knew him well enough to believe that, although he had retired from office, his successor and the party genentlly could have his valuable co-operation. The proposition was unanimously passed. Mr. PEACOCK, iii returning thanks for his election, said he did not under-estimate the importance of the position. He had held the vice-chairmanship for four years and had always endeavoured to put Liberalism first and his own private interests afterwards. He had had Councillor Wilkinson's assurance that he could count upon his support, and they hoped during the coming year to do such affective work for Liberalism as would help to place Mr. Crostleld at the head of the poll at the next election. (Applause.) Mr. Peacock I then proposed the election of Councillor Wilkinson as deputy-chairman. The proposition was cordially agreed to. Councillor WILKINSON, in reply, said his heart and soul would always be with the Liberal Association and with the work of Liberalism in general. (Applause). FINANCE COMMITTEE. In addition to one delegate from each ward, the following co-optative members of the Finance Com- mittee were elected :—Alderman Tinnion, Councillor Halli well, Messrs Joseph Wilkinson, George Weaver. J E Worrell, J Griffiths, W Bibby, G Walker, Firth Lindley, j' Delaney, and E Wynne Jones.

REPRESENTATIVE ON EXECUTIVE. It was decided that the Women's Liberal Association the Junior Liberal Association and the Progressive Labour League shonld each nominate two representa- tives on the Executive Committee.

AUDITOR THANKED. A vote of thanks was passed to Mr J G Chappell for auditing the accounts.

BANK OFFICIAL'S RETIREMENT.

MR. WM. SILCOCK'S LONG RECORD.

Mr. William Silcock, chief cashier at Parr's Bank' Warrington, has this week retired after forty-eight years' service and the event has been fittingly recognized by a handsome presentation from his colleagues. Mr. Silcock is a native of Warrington and has spent the whole of his life in the "good old town." A on of the late Mr William Silcock, wire manufacturer (who represented the old south-west ward on the Town Connell and was one of the first directors of the Warrington Waterworks Company) he was born in September, 1846. His education was received at Winwick Grammar School, of which the Rev Samuel Burnell, M.A., was the headmaster. Mr Silcock entered the service of Messrs Parr and Co. in 1864 and has eeen the rise and progress of the bank through a long and varied period. In those early days the bank had two branches; now it is one. of the foremost banking institutions of the day, with over 300 branches and important agencies all over the world. When Mr. Silcock first became connected with the bank, it was under the management of the partners and afterwards, before the removal of the head office to London, the late Mr. Dun had charge. He was suceeded by Mr Murray and the present manager is Mr. W. Skinner.

Mr. Si!cock has taken a prominent part in the social and educational interests of the town. He is President of the Literary and Philosophical Society and of the Warrington Cricket and Bowling Club and has also rendered good service on the Council of the Beautiful Warrington :Society. Be is a member of the Wesleyan denomination and has held many impertant offices, including that of superintendent of the Sunday school at Bold-street.

On Wednesday evening the members of the War- rington staff of the bank were the guests of the manager (Mr. W. Skinner), at his residence, Hill. Top, Latchford, and the occasion was marked by a presentation to Mr. Silcock. The gift took the form of a solid silver rose bowl inscribed as follows "Presented to Mr. William Silcock by past and present colleagues of Parr's Bank, Warrington, on his retirement after forty-eight years seryice." The subscribers included not only the whole of the Warrington staff but the chief officials at the , head office in London. Mr. Skinner made the presentation and paid a very high tribute to Mr. Silcock's sterling character and faithful services. Mr J Bernard (sub-manager) added a few well-chosen words and Mr. Silcock, in acknowledging the gift, gave some interesting reminiscences. An enjoyable programme was submitted iicluding songs by Mr H Dixon and Mr Lord, and recitati,ms by Ur W H Brown and Mr T Deakin. A vote of thanks was passed to Mr and Mrs Skinner for their hospitality, on the proposition of Mr J C Macdonald, a happy gathering concluding with " Auld Lange Syne." Mr. I. W. Turton, of Liverpool, a former member of the Warrington staff, will succeed Mr. Silcock as chief cashier.

WARRINGTON SOCIETY.

Memorial Tablets to Mrs. Barbauld and Warrington Bridge.

The annual meeting of the Warrington Society was held in the Old Academy on lhuraday evening. Mr E J Hall presided over an attendance of 35 members, and at the outset read letters of apology from the Mayor, Alderman Bennett and Mr Hobert Garnett. The election of officers was then proceeded with. Mr William Long, J.P. was re-elected president. Mr John Fairhnrst resigned the bon treasurership, and a hearty vote of thanks was passed to him for his valuable services. Mr J Foster Jackson was elected to succeed him. Alderman Bennett and Mr James Richardson, jun., were re-eltcted joint secretaries, and Mr Bradshaw was appointed auditor. The following gentlemen form the General Purposes Committee: Messrs G W A Brown, R G S Finney, It Garnett, P C Garnett, L Greening, E J Hall, A T Hallaway, F A Harris, J Fairhurst, J W Slack, J Smith, sad H Grosvenor. In the absence of Aldermen Bennett, Mr. J. IticiwiDsoN, jun., submitted the annual !report which gave an inieresting review of the local events of the yast year. The committee, it was stated, decided to erect a tablet on the site of the residence of Miss Anna Laetitia Aikin (Mrs. Barbauld) no, 8, Dial-street, in commemoration of the fact that she spent fifteen of the happiest years of her life in the -old town, and to mark the centenary of the publication of her famous poem "Eighteen , Hundred and Eleven." It was decided to offer a I premium of two guineas to the student of the School of Art who submitted the most suitable design and this was awarded to Mr. J. A. Allen. The tablet had been beautifully cast in bronze, and would be fixed in the hammered iron framework erected above the gateway of the house which now occupies the site. With regard to the new Warrington Bridge, the report urged upon the Town Council the extreme importance of preserving the spaciousness of its approaches, and the desirability of commemorating the history of its six predecessors by suitable decorative tablets.

WARRINGTON UNION TENDERS.— PERSONS WILLING to Contract for the supply of any of the undermentioned articles, to be delivered free of expense at the Workhouse,Warrington, or Cottage Homes, Pad gate, at such times and in such quantities as the Guardians or their officers may require, are requested to send in tenders to me not later than 5 p.m. on Tuesday the 12th March, 1912, and the same will be considered by the Guardians at it meeting to be held on Wednesday, the 13th March, 1912. From lot April, 1912, to 31st March, 1913. Uniforms for Male Officers. Milk—(Workhouse and Cottage Homes). Alternate prices to be quoted for 1 year's supply and 3 years' supply from lot April, 1912. From Ist April, 1912, to 30th September, 1912. Coal and Slack Groceries and Necessaries Clogs Butchers' Meat Crockery Thirds Boots, Shoes and Slippers Flour and Oatmeal Coffins Bread for Cottage Homes Brooms and Brushes Drapery Leather and Shoemakers' Wines and Spirits Necessaries Beer and Stout Tailors' Goods Men's Suits Butter,Bacon,Choese,Lard Ironmongery and Margarine Macintosh Sheeting. Cutlery Drugs Tobacco and Snuff Dressings Fish;and Ice Soda Water Tea Paints, &c. Separate Tenders must be sent for the Workhouse and Cottage Homes, and Samples of the articles required can be seen at either Institution between the hours of 10 a.m. and 4 p.m. The fuel to be supplied must be according to the specification as set forth on the tender form, and quota- tions must be made for delivery either to the new siding adjoining the Workhouse or by cart, and by cart to the Cottage Homes. Forms of tender, and instructions to persons tender- ing (which must be strictly complied with) may be , obtained from the undersigned. All accounts will be paid monthly. The Guardians do not necessarily bind themselves to accept the lowest or any tender. ' By Order, ARTHUR BOTTOMLEY, Clerk to the Guardians. Bewsey Chambers, Warrington, 23rd February, 1912.

country. Again Councillor Roberts sets him right by pointing out that for 1909 we imported £130,000 less than we exported. A third mis-statement was cor- rected in the House by Mr. T. C. Taylor,M.P. for Radcliffe, in regard to the woollen industry, which Mr. Smith said was in danger owing to our fiscal policy. As a matter of fact our imports have gone down from five million yards per year to two and three quarter million yards, while our exports have gone up from 48 million yards, to 861/2 million yards. This is a typical Tarff Reform threatened industry, and the argument is typical of Mr. Harold. Smith.

RYLANDS W.R.C.H. v RUNCORN TALLY HO. Thirty runners rePresenting these clubs turned out from the Recreation Ground, Goreey-lane, and covered a fast course of about six miles at racing pace J Clarke and T Jones maintained a merry gait for about three miles when the latter lost his place and the Royal BILIA of Itylands' was very prominent in the closing stages of the race which, owing to good packing by the wire men resulted in their favour by 18 points. RYLANDS. RUNCORN. C A Harding J Thorniley 3 F Walker A Whitmore I CE Escott E A Burton Warburton 8 T Jones ll II Woods 31 49

Warrington A.C. had a fair muster of novices to cover a five-mile course while their national team spun merrily over about nine miles of good going. W J Bradbury headed the novice section followed by G Wakefield. A Siddall, W Bolton, T Turton, 1 Parsonage, F Shad well, and T Rowlinson were the leading section whilst H K Jepson, E Scott, C Vase. F Antrobus, H Holbrook and W Antrubus came through at a steady pace. Today's fixture is the all importint National Championship at Haydock Park, starting at 3.30 p.m., train leaves Central Station at 2.10 p.m., and as 19 of the best teams (about 223 competitors) in the country have entered, a stirring contest is certain. The holders, Hallamshire, have good reason for confidence, but the Warrington lads will do their best at shaking it. Ilibbins, Glover and Neaves have their followers for the individual honour, Scott and Wallach, if they run, will have theirs, but the locals are hopeful of having a good say in the argument, which is looked upon as a test for the Olympic games.

A CUP FOR BOWLERS. At the close of the meeting a handsome silver cup was placed on the table and it was announced that Mr Peter Peacock had presented it for cornpetion amongst the various ward committees during the coming summer. The PRESIDENT said he was sure the Liberal Associa- tion as a whole appreciated very highly that tangible token of Mr. Peacock's interest in Liberalism, and the cup would add a great deal of zest to the games during the ensuing season. (Applause.) Mr. J. CRITCRLEY moved a vote of thanks to Mr. Peacock for his generosity and said the cup would be an incentive to the general body of workers to renewed activity. Alderman TINNION seconded. He remarked that the social aide of politics, as well as the intellectual and constructive aide, was well worth cultivating, and it was a happy thought on Mr. Peacock's part to present such a handsome gift. (Applause). The vote was passed with acclamation. Mr. PEAcocrt,in reply, said his object was to stimulate the workers of the party to greater efforts for the cause which they loved so much. (Applause). The Chairman was thanked for presiding, on the proposition Of Mr. W. BIBBY, seconded by Mr. AL HOWARTH.
